Global stability of Minkowski spacetime with minimal decay
Dawei Shen

Abstract
The global stability of Minkowski spacetime, a milestone in the field, has been proven in the celebrated work of Christodoulou and Klainerman [ 5 ] in 1993. In 2007, Bieri [ 1 ] has extended the result of [ 5 ] under lower decay and regularity assumptions on the initial data. In this paper, we extend the result of [ 1 ] to minimal decay assumptions. Also, concerning the treatment of curvature estimates, we replace the vectorfield method used in [ 5 , 1 ] by the r p r^{p} –weighted estimates of Dafermos and Rodnianski [ 6 ] . Keywords Minkowski stability, Maximal-null foliation, r p r^{p} –weighted estimates



1
Introduction

1.1
Einstein vacuum equations and the Cauchy problem

A Lorentzian
4
–manifold
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
is called a vacuum spacetime if it solves the Einstein vacuum equations:

{\mathbf{Ric}}(\mathbf{g})=0\quad\mbox{ in }\mathcal{M},

(1.1)

where
{\mathbf{Ric}}
denotes the Ricci tensor of the Lorentzian metric
\mathbf{g}
. The Einstein vacuum equations are invariant under diffeomorphisms, and therefore one considers equivalence classes of solutions. Expressed in general coordinates, (
1.1
) is a non-linear geometric coupled system of partial differential equations of order 2 for
\mathbf{g}
. In suitable coordinates, for example so-called wave coordinates, it can be shown that (
1.1
) is hyperbolic and hence admits an initial value formulation.

The corresponding initial data for the Einstein vacuum equations is given by specifying a triplet
(\Sigma,g,k)
where
(\Sigma,g)
is a Riemannian
3
–manifold and
k
is the traceless symmetric
2
–tensor on
\Sigma
satisfying the constraint equations:

\displaystyle\begin{split}R&=|k|^{2}-(\tr k)^{2},\\
\nabla^{j}k_{ij}&=\nabla_{i}\tr k,\end{split}

(1.2)

where
R
denotes the scalar curvature of
g
,
\nabla
denotes the Levi-Civita connection of
g
and

\displaystyle|k|^{2}:=g^{im}g^{jl}k_{ij}k_{lm},\qquad\quad\tr k:=g^{ij}k_{ij}.

In the future development
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
of such initial data
(\Sigma,g,k)
,
\Sigma\subset\mathcal{M}
is a spacelike hypersurface with induced metric
g
and second fundamental form
k
.

The seminal well-posedness results for the Cauchy problem obtained in
[
3
,
4
]
ensure that for any smooth Cauchy data, there exists a unique smooth maximal globally hyperbolic development
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
solution of Einstein equations (
1.1
) such that
\Sigma\subset\mathcal{M}
and
g
,
k
are respectively the first and second fundamental forms of
\Sigma
in
\mathcal{M}
.

The prime example of a vacuum spacetime is Minkowski space
(\mathcal{M},\eta)
:

\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}^{4},\qquad\eta=-(dt)^{2}+(dx^{1})^{2}+(dx^{2})^{2}+(dx^{3})^{2},

for which Cauchy data are given by

\Sigma=\mathbb{R}^{3},\qquad g=(dx^{1})^{2}+(dx^{2})^{2}+(dx^{3})^{2},\qquad k=0.

In the present work, we consider the problem of the stability of Minkowski spacetime and start by reviewing the state of the art on this problem.

1.2
Previous works on the stability of Minkowski spacetime

In 1993, Christodoulou and Klainerman
[
5
]
proved the global stability of Minkowski for the Einstein-vacuum equations, a milestone in the domain of mathematical general relativity. In 2007, Bieri
[
1
]
gave a new proof of global stability of Minkowski requiring one less derivative and less vectorfields compared to
[
5
]
. Both
[
5
]
and
[
1
]
rely on the maximal foliation. Given that the goal of this paper is to extend the result of
[
1
]
, we will state the results of
[
5
,
1
]
in Section
1.3
.

We now mention proofs of Minkowski stability using other gauges. In 2003, Klainerman and Nicolò
[
15
]
proved the Minkowski stability in the exterior of an outgoing cone using the double null foliation. Moreover, Klainerman and Nicolò
[
16
]
showed that under stronger asymptotic decay and regularity properties than those used in
[
5
,
15
]
, asymptotically flat initial data sets lead to solutions of the Einstein vacuum equations which have strong peeling properties. Lindblad and Rodnianski
[
20
,
21
]
gave a new proof of the stability of the Minkowski spacetime using
wave-coordinates
and showing that the Einstein equations verify the so called
weak null structure
in that gauge. Huneau
[
13
]
proved the nonlinear stability of Minkowski spacetime with a translation Killing field using generalised wave-coordinates. Using the framework of Melrose’s b-analysis, Hintz and Vasy
[
11
]
reproved the stability of Minkowski space. Graf
[
9
]
proved the global nonlinear stability of Minkowski space in the context of the spacelike-characteristic Cauchy problem for Einstein vacuum equations, which together with
[
15
]
allows to reobtain
[
5
]
. Under the framework of
[
15
]
and using
r^{p}
–weighted estimates of Dafermos and Rodnianski
[
6
]
, the author
[
25
]
reproved the Minkowski stability in exterior regions. More recently, Hintz
[
10
]
reproved the Minkowski stability in exterior regions by using the framework of
[
11
]
.

There are also stability results concerning Einstein’s equations coupled with non trivial matter fields:

•

Einstein-Maxwell system: Zipser
[
30
]
extended the framework of
[
5
]
to show the stability of the Minkowski spacetime solution to the Einstein–Maxwell system. In
[
23
]
, Loizelet used the framework of
[
21
]
to demonstrate the stability of the Minkowski spacetime solution of the Einstein-scalar field-Maxwell system in
(1+n)
-dimensions
(n\geq 3)
. Speck
[
26
]
gave a proof of the global nonlinear stability of the
(1+3)
-dimensional Minkowski spacetime solution to the coupled system for a family of electromagnetic fields, which includes the standard Maxwell fields.

•

Einstein-Klein-Gordon system: Lefloch and Ma
[
19
]
and Wang
[
28
]
proved the global stability of Minkowski for the Einstein-Klein-Gordon system with initial data coinciding with the Schwarzschild solution with small mass outside a compact set. Ionescu and Pausader
[
14
]
proved the global stability of Minkowski for the Einstein-Klein-Gordon system for general initial data.

•

Einstein-Vlasov system: Taylor
[
27
]
considered the massless case where the initial data for the Vlasov part is compactly supported on the mass shell. Fajman, Joudioux and Smulevici
[
7
]
considered the massive case where the initial data coincides with Schwarzschild in the exterior region and with compact support assumption only in space on the Vlasov part. Lindblad and Taylor
[
22
]
considered the massive case where the initial data has compact support for the Vlasov part. Bigorgne, Fajman, Joudioux, Smulevici and Thaller
[
2
]
considered the massless case for general initial data. Wang
[
29
]
considered the massive case for general initial data.

1.3
Minkowski stability in
[
5
,
1
]

We recall in this section the results in
[
5
,
1
]
. First, we recall the definition of a
maximal hypersurface
, which plays an important role in the statements of the main theorems in
[
5
,
1
]
.

Definition 1.1
.

An initial data
(\Sigma,g,k)
is posed on a maximal hypersurface if it satisfies

\tr k=0.

(1.3)

In this case, we say that
(\Sigma,g,k)
is a maximal initial data set, and the constraint equations (
1.2
) reduce to

R=|k|^{2},\qquad\sdiv k=0,\qquad\tr k=0.

(1.4)

We introduce the notion of
(s,q)
–asymptotically flat initial data
.

Definition 1.2
.

Given
s>1
and
q\in\mathbb{N}
, we say that a data set
(\Sigma_{0},g,k)
is
(s,q)
–asymptotically flat if there exists a coordinate system
(x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})
defined outside a sufficiently large compact set such that:

•

In the case
s\geq 3
1
1

1

The notation
f=o_{q}(r^{-m})
means
\partial^{\alpha}f=o(r^{-m-|\alpha|})
,
|\alpha|\leq q
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}g_{ij}&=\left(1-\frac{2M}{r}\right)^{-1}dr^{2}+r^{2}d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}+o_{q+1}(r^{-\frac{s-1}{2}}),\\
k_{ij}&=o_{q}(r^{-\frac{s+1}{2}}).\end{split}

(1.5)

•

In the case
1<s<3

\displaystyle\begin{split}g_{ij}&=\delta_{ij}+o_{q+1}(r^{-\frac{s-1}{2}}),\\
k_{ij}&=o_{q}(r^{-\frac{s+1}{2}}).\end{split}

(1.6)

Remark 1.3
.

According to (
1.6
), the assumption
s>1
is necessary to define an asymptotic perturbation of the flat metric on
\Sigma_{0}
. See also Remark
3.7
for more explanations.

Definition 1.4
.

We denote
d_{0}
the geodesic distance from a fixed point
O\in\Sigma_{0}
,
B_{ij}:=(\curl\widehat{{R}})_{ij}
the
Bach tensor
and
\widehat{{R}}
is the traceless part of
\sRic
. Then, we define for
q\geq 2
:

•

In the case
s\geq 3
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}J_{0}^{(q)}(\Sigma_{0},g,k)&:=\sup_{\Sigma_{0}}\Big((d_{0}^{2}+1)^{3}|\sRic|^{2}\Big)+\sum_{l=0}^{q}\int_{\Sigma_{0}}(d_{0}^{2}+1)^{l+\frac{s-2}{2}}|\nabla^{l}k|^{2}\\
&+\sum_{l=0}^{q-2}\int_{\Sigma_{0}}(d_{0}^{2}+1)^{l+\frac{s+2}{2}}|\nabla^{l}B|^{2}.\end{split}

(1.7)

•

In the case
1<s<3
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}J_{0}^{(q)}(\Sigma_{0},g,k)&:=\sum_{l=0}^{q}\int_{\Sigma_{0}}(d_{0}^{2}+1)^{l+\frac{s-2}{2}}|\nabla^{l}k|^{2}+\sum_{l=0}^{q-1}\int_{\Sigma_{0}}(d_{0}^{2}+1)^{l+\frac{s}{2}}|\nabla^{l}\sRic|^{2}.\end{split}

(1.8)

We can now state the main theorems of
[
5
]
and
[
1
]
.

Theorem 1.5

(Global stability of Minkowski spacetime
[
5
]
)
.

There exists an
\varepsilon_{0}>0
sufficiently small such that if
J_{0}^{(3)}(\Sigma_{0},g,k)\leq\varepsilon_{0}^{2}
, then the initial data set
(\Sigma_{0},g,k)
,
(4,3)
–asymptotically flat (in the sense of Definition
1.2
) and maximal, has a unique, globally hyperbolic, smooth, geodesically complete solution. This development is globally asymptotically flat, i.e. the Riemann curvature tensor tends to zero along any causal or space-like geodesic. Moreover, there exists a global maximal time function
t
and an optical function
2
2

2

An optical function
u
is a scalar function satisfying
\mathbf{g}^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}u\partial_{\beta}u=0
.

u
defined everywhere in an external region.

Theorem 1.6

(Global stability of Minkowski spacetime
[
1
]
)
.

There exists an
\varepsilon_{0}>0
sufficiently small such that if
J_{0}^{(2)}(\Sigma_{0},g,k)\leq\varepsilon_{0}^{2}
, then the initial data set
(\Sigma_{0},g,k)
,
(2,2)
–asymptotically flat and maximal, has a unique, globally hyperbolic, smooth, geodesically complete solution. This development is globally asymptotically flat. Moreover, there exists a global maximal time function
t
and an optical function
u
defined everywhere in an external region.

Remark 1.7
.

Various choices of the parameter
s
in Definition
1.2
have been made in the literature. In particular:
3
3

3

This list focuses on low values of
s
. For large values of
s
, note for example
[
16
]
valid for
s>7
and
[
25
]
valid for
s>3
.

•

s=4
is used for example in
[
5
]
.

•

s=3+\delta
4
4

4

Here and in the line below,
\delta
denotes a constant satisfying
0<\delta\ll 1
.
is used for example in
[
21
]
.

•

s=3-\delta
is used in
[
14
]
for the Einstein-Klein-Gordon system.

•

s=2
is used in
[
1
]
.

The goal of this paper is to extend the results of
[
1
]
to lower values of
s
. More precisely, we prove the global stability of Minkowski spacetime for
(s,2)
–asymptotically flat initial data for all
s\in(1,2]
.

1.4
Rough version of the main theorem

In this section, we state a simple version of our main theorem. For the precise statement, see Theorem
3.6
.

Theorem 1.8

(Main Theorem, first version)
.

Let
s\in(1,2]
and let an initial data set
(\Sigma_{0},g,k)
which is
(s,2)
–asymptotically flat in the sense of Definition
1.2
. Assume that we have a smallness conditions in an initial layer region
\mathcal{V}_{(0)}
5
5

5

See (
2.9
) for the definition of
\mathcal{V}_{(0)}
.
near
\Sigma_{0}
. Then, there exists a unique future development
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
in its future domain of dependence with the following properties:

•

(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
can be foliated by a maximal-null foliation
(C_{u},\Sigma_{t})
whose outgoing leaves
C_{u}
are complete for all
u
;

•

We have detailed control of all the quantities associated with the maximal-null foliation of the spacetime, see Theorem
3.6
.

Remark 1.9
.

In the particular case
s=2
, we reobtain the results of
[
1
]
restated above in Theorem
1.6
.

The structure of the proof of Theorem
1.8
is similar to
[
5
,
1
]
, see Section
3.5
. Below, we compare the proof of this paper and that of
[
1
]
:

1.

In
[
1
]
, the functional (
1.8
) is introduced to fix the initial conditions on the initial hypersurface
\Sigma_{0}
. Here we fix the initial conditions in an initial layer region
\mathcal{V}_{(0)}
near the initial hypersurface
\Sigma_{0}
.

2.

To estimate the norms of curvature components,
[
1
]
uses the vectorfield method introduced in
[
5
]
. Here, we estimate the curvature norms by relying on
r^{p}
-weighted estimates, a method introduced by Dafermos and Rodnianski in
[
6
]
. This allows for a simpler treatment of the curvature estimates.

3.

In order to obtain optimal decay for all the connection coefficients,
[
1
]
uses two foliations constructed respectively forward and backward. In this paper, we derive decay for the connection coefficients based on only one foliation constructed forward from the initial hypersurface
\Sigma_{0}
and the symmetry axis
\varPhi
. This avoids having to compare forward and backward gauges.

1.5
Structure of the paper

•

In Section
2
, we introduce the geometric set-up and the basic equations.

•

In Section
3
, we state a precise version of the main theorem. We then state intermediate results, and use them to prove the main theorem. The rest of the paper then focuses on the proof of these intermediary results.

•

In Section
4
, we make bootstrap assumptions and prove first consequences. These consequences will be used frequently in the rest of the paper.

•

In Section
5
, we apply
r^{p}
–weighted estimates to Bianchi equations to control curvature.

•

In Section
6
, we estimate maximal connection coefficients and the lapse function using the elliptic systems satisfied on maximal hypersurfaces.

•

In Section
7
, we estimate the null connection coefficients using the null structure equations.

•

In Appendix
A
, we prove Theorem
5.8
, which provides a unified treatment of nonlinear error terms appearing in the curvature estimates of Section
5
.

1.6
Acknowledgements

The author is very grateful to Jérémie Szeftel for his support, discussions, encouragements and patient guidance.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Geometric set-up

2.1.1
Maximal foliation

We foliate the spacetime
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
by spacelike hypersurfaces
\Sigma_{t}
as level hypersurfaces of a time function
t
. We denote by
T
the unit, future oriented, normal to
\Sigma_{t}
. We then introduce a coordinates system
(x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})
on
\Sigma_{0}
, and extend it to
\mathcal{M}
by

\displaystyle T(x^{i})=0,\qquad\quad i=1,2,3.

Relative to this foliation, the spacetime metric takes the form:

\mathbf{g}=-\phi^{2}dt^{2}+\sum_{i,j=1}^{3}g_{ij}dx^{i}dx^{j}.

(2.1)

We define the
lapse function
of the foliation by

\phi(t,x):=(\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{D}t,\mathbf{D}t))^{-1}.

Then, we have

\displaystyle T=\phi\mathbf{D}t.

We define the second fundamental form:

\displaystyle k_{ij}:=-\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{D}_{i}T,\partial_{j}).

(2.2)

We denote by
\nabla
and
\sRic
respectively the induced covariant derivative and the corresponding Ricci curvature tensor on the leaves
\Sigma_{t}
. Relative to an orthonormal frame
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}
tangent to the leaves of the foliation, we have the formulae:

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{i}e_{j}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{i}e_{j}-k_{ij}T,

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{i}T

\displaystyle=-k_{ij}e_{j},

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{T}e_{i}

\displaystyle=(\phi^{-1}\nabla_{i}\phi)T,

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{T}T

\displaystyle=(\phi^{-1}\nabla_{i}\phi)e_{i},

where
\nabla_{T}e_{i}
denotes the projection of
\mathbf{D}_{T}e_{i}
to the tangent space of
\Sigma_{t}
.

Lemma 2.1
.

We have the following structure equations of the foliation:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}k_{ij}

\displaystyle=-\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\phi+\phi({\mathbf{R}}_{iTjT}-k_{il}{k^{l}}_{j}),

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}k_{jl}-\nabla_{j}k_{il}

\displaystyle={\mathbf{R}}_{lTij},

\displaystyle\sRic_{ij}-k_{il}{k^{l}}_{j}+k_{ij}\tr k

\displaystyle={\mathbf{R}}_{iTjT}+{\mathbf{R}}_{ij}.

Proof.

See (1.0.3a)–(1.0.3c) in
[
5
]
.
∎

Taking the trace of the formulae in Lemma
2.1
, we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 2.2
.

If
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
is a solution of Einstein equations, we have the following equations:

\displaystyle R-|k|^{2}+(\tr k)^{2}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\nabla^{j}k_{ji}-\nabla^{i}\tr k

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\tr k

\displaystyle=-\Delta\phi+\phi|k|^{2}.

Combining Lemma
2.1
and Corollary
2.2
, we obtain the following proposition.

Proposition 2.3
.

We impose a maximal foliation on
\Sigma_{t}
in the sense of Definition
1.1
. Then, we have:

•

Maximal constraint equations:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tr k&=0,\\
\nabla^{j}k_{ji}&=0,\\
R&=|k|^{2}.\end{split}

(2.3)

•

Evolution equations:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{t}g_{ij}&=-2\phi k_{ij},\\
\partial_{t}k_{ij}&=-\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\phi+\phi(\sRic_{ij}-2k_{il}{k^{l}}_{j}).\end{split}

(2.4)

•

Lapse equation:

\displaystyle\Delta\phi=\phi|k|^{2}.

(2.5)

Proof.

See (1.0.11)–(1.0.13) in
[
5
]
.
∎

2.1.2
Maximal-null foliation

Let
O\in\Sigma_{0}
. We define
\varPhi
as the integral curve of
O
along the vectorfield
T
, called the
symmetry axis
. Starting from
O
, we construct an outgoing null cone
C_{0}
in the future of
\Sigma_{0}
. The spacetime
\mathcal{M}
is then divided into

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}=I^{+}(O)\cup C_{0}\cup D^{+}(\Sigma_{0}\setminus O),

where
I^{+}(O)
denotes the
future domain of influence
of
O
and
D^{+}(\Sigma_{0}\setminus O)
denotes the
future domain of dependence
of
\Sigma_{0}\setminus O
.

We now construct an outgoing optical function
u
in
\mathcal{M}
as follows. First, to allow ourselves some room, we assume that a spacetime slab
\bigcup_{t\in[-1,0]}\Sigma_{t}
in the past of
\Sigma_{0}
has been constructed. We denote

\displaystyle O_{-1}:=\varPhi\cap\Sigma_{-1}.

We define
u
on
\varPhi
by

T(u)=1\quad\mbox{on }\varPhi,\qquad\quad u(O)=0.

Then, for any
p\in\varPhi\cap I^{+}(O_{-1})
, we construct, emanating from
p
, an outgoing null cone
C_{u(p)}
. Thus, the spacetime
I^{+}(O_{-1})
is foliated by the outgoing null cones
C_{u}
for
u>u(O_{-1})
. We then extend
u
to
I^{+}(O_{-1})
by assuming that
C_{u}
are level sets of
u
and we denote

\displaystyle L:=-\grad u\quad\mbox{ in }\;I^{+}(O_{-1}).

Note that

\displaystyle I^{+}(O)\cup C_{0}\subseteq I^{+}(O_{-1}).

It thus remains to define
u
in
D^{+}(\Sigma_{0}\setminus O)
.

For
u(O_{-1})<u\leq 0
, the
2
–spheres
C_{u}\cap\Sigma_{0}
define a radial foliation on
\Sigma_{0}\cap I^{+}(O_{-1})
centered at
O
. We define a scalar function
w
on
\Sigma_{0}
by assuming

\displaystyle w:=-u,\qquad\mbox{ on }\Sigma_{0}\cap I^{+}(O_{-1}),

and extending it smoothly to
\Sigma_{0}
. We thus fixed a radial foliation on the initial hypersurface
\Sigma_{0}
centered at
O
by the level sets of
w
. The corresponding leaves are denoted by

S_{(0)}(w_{1})=\{p\in\Sigma_{0}\big/\,w(p)=w_{1}\},

(2.6)

where
w_{1}\geq 0
and
w(O)=0
. Then, we define the outgoing optical function
u
in
D^{+}(\Sigma_{0}\setminus O)
by the following
Eikonal equation
:

\mathbf{g}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}u\partial_{\nu}u=0,\qquad\quad u|_{\Sigma_{0}\setminus O}=-w.

(2.7)

Denoting

\displaystyle L:=-\grad u\qquad\mbox{ in }D^{+}(\Sigma_{0}\setminus K),

we have the following geodesic equation:

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{L}L=0.

Thus,
u
and
L
are well-defined everywhere in
\mathcal{M}
. Moreover,
u
is smooth in
\mathcal{M}
by construction.

We consider the points on
\varPhi
:

\displaystyle S(t,u_{c}(t)):=\Sigma_{t}\cap\varPhi,\qquad S(t_{c}(u),u):=C_{u}\cap\varPhi,

(2.8)

as spheres of radius
0
. Thus, the spacetime
\mathcal{M}
is foliated by
2
–spheres:

\displaystyle S(t,u):=\Sigma_{t}\cap C_{u},\qquad u\in\mathbb{R},\quad t\geq 0.

We define the
initial layer region
:

\mathcal{V}_{(0)}:=\mathcal{M}\cap\{0\leq t\leq 2\}=J^{-}(\Sigma_{2})\cap J^{+}(\Sigma_{0}),

(2.9)

where
J^{\pm}(\Sigma)
denotes the
causal future
(resp.
past
) of
\Sigma
. Note that we have

\displaystyle u>0\;\;\mbox{ in }I^{+}(O),\qquad u=0\;\;\mbox{ on }C_{0},\qquad u<0\;\;\mbox{ in }D^{+}(\Sigma_{0}\setminus O),

see Figure
1
for a picture.

Figure 1:
Maximal-null foliation of the spacetime
\mathcal{M}

We also define for
t\geq 2

V_{t}:=J^{-}(\Sigma_{t})\cap J^{+}(\Sigma_{2}).

For any
t\geq 2
, we define the
exterior region
:

\displaystyle{{}^{e}V}_{t}:=\{p\in V_{t}\big/\,u\leq 1\},

and the
interior region
:

\displaystyle{{}^{i}V}_{t}:=\{p\in V_{t}\big/\,u\geq 1\}.

We then define the null vectorfield
e_{4}
and the
null lapse function

a
by

a:=|\nabla u|^{-1},\qquad\quad e_{4}:=aL.

By definition, we have

\displaystyle 0=\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{D}u,\mathbf{D}u)=|\nabla u|^{2}-|T(u)|^{2}=a^{-2}-|T(u)|^{2},

which implies

\displaystyle a=T(u)^{-1}.

Thus, we have in particular
a=1
on
\varPhi
. Next, we define

N:=e_{4}-T,

which is a vectorfield on
\mathcal{M}\setminus\varPhi
. By a direct computation, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}(T,N)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{g}(T,e_{4}-T)=a\mathbf{g}(T,L)-\mathbf{g}(T,T)=-a\mathbf{g}(T,\mathbf{D}u)+1=-aT(u)+1=0,

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}(N,N)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{g}(e_{4}-T,e_{4}-T)=-2\mathbf{g}(e_{4},T)+\mathbf{g}(T,T)=-2a\mathbf{g}(L,T)-1=2aT(u)-1=1.

Thus,
N
denotes the unit vectorfield tangent to
\Sigma_{t}
, oriented towards infinity and orthogonal to the leaves
S(t,u)
.

We denote by
\theta
the second fundamental form of the surfaces
S(t,u)
relative to
\Sigma_{t}
:

\theta_{AB}:=\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{D}_{A}N,e_{B}).

We then define the null vectorfield
e_{3}
by

e_{3}:=T-N.

By a direct computation, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}(e_{3},e_{3})=\mathbf{g}(T-N,T-N)=0,\qquad\quad\mathbf{g}(e_{3},e_{4})=\mathbf{g}(T-N,T+N)=0.

On a given
2
–sphere
S(t,u)
, we choose a local orthonormal frame
(e_{1},e_{2})
and we call
(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4})
a null frame.
6
6

6

Note that the null frame
(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4})
is well-defined on
\mathcal{M}\setminus\varPhi
.

Remark 2.4
.

As a convention, throughout the paper, we use capital Latin letters
A,B,C,...
to denote an index from
1
to
2
, lower case Latin letters
i,j,k,...
to denote an index from
1
to
3
and Greek letters
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,...
to denote an index from
1
to
4
, e.g.
e_{A}
denotes either
e_{1}
or
e_{2}
.

The spacetime metric
\mathbf{g}
induces a Riemannian metric
\not{g}
on
S(t,u)
. We use
\not{\nabla}
to denote the Levi-Civita connection of
\not{g}
on
S(t,u)
. We recall the null decomposition of the Ricci coefficients and curvature components of the null frame
(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4})
as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\underline{\chi}_{AB}&=\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{D}_{A}e_{3},e_{B}),\qquad\quad\chi_{AB}=\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{D}_{A}e_{4},e_{B}),\\
\underline{\xi}_{A}&=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{D}_{3}e_{3},e_{A}),\qquad\quad\,\,\xi_{A}=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{D}_{4}e_{4},e_{A}),\\
\underline{\omega}&=\frac{1}{4}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{D}_{3}e_{3},e_{4}),\qquad\quad\,\,\,\;\,\omega=\frac{1}{4}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{D}_{4}e_{4},e_{3}),\\
\underline{\eta}_{A}&=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{D}_{4}e_{3},e_{A}),\qquad\quad\;\eta_{A}=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{D}_{3}e_{4},e_{A}),\\
\zeta_{A}&=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{D}_{e_{A}}e_{4},e_{3}),\end{split}

(2.10)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\alpha_{AB}&={\mathbf{R}}(e_{A},e_{4},e_{B},e_{4}),\qquad\,\,\,\underline{\alpha}_{AB}={\mathbf{R}}(e_{A},e_{3},e_{B},e_{3}),\\
\beta_{A}&=\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{R}}(e_{A},e_{4},e_{3},e_{4}),\qquad\,\,\;\underline{\beta}_{A}=\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{R}}(e_{A},e_{3},e_{3},e_{4}),\\
\rho&=\frac{1}{4}{\mathbf{R}}(e_{3},e_{4},e_{3},e_{4}),\qquad\,\;\;\,\,\,\,\sigma=\frac{1}{4}{{}^{*}{\mathbf{R}}}(e_{3},e_{4},e_{3},e_{4}),\end{split}

(2.11)

where
{}^{*}{\mathbf{R}}
denotes the Hodge dual of
{\mathbf{R}}
.

The null second fundamental forms
\chi,\underline{\chi}
are further decomposed in their traces
\tr\chi
and
\tr\underline{\chi}
, and traceless parts
\widehat{\chi}
and
\widehat{\underline{\chi}}
:

\displaystyle\tr\chi

\displaystyle:=\not{g}^{AB}\chi_{AB},\qquad\quad\,\widehat{\chi}_{AB}:=\chi_{AB}-\frac{1}{2}\not{g}_{AB}\tr\chi,

\displaystyle\tr\underline{\chi}

\displaystyle:=\not{g}^{AB}\underline{\chi}_{AB},\qquad\quad\,\widehat{\underline{\chi}}_{AB}:=\underline{\chi}_{AB}-\frac{1}{2}\not{g}_{AB}\tr\underline{\chi}.

We define the horizontal covariant operator
\not{\nabla}
as follows:

\not{\nabla}_{X}Y:=\mathbf{D}_{X}Y-\frac{1}{2}\underline{\chi}(X,Y)e_{4}-\frac{1}{2}\chi(X,Y)e_{3}.

We also define
\not{\nabla}_{4}X
and
\not{\nabla}_{3}X
to be the horizontal projections of
\mathbf{D}_{4}X
and
\mathbf{D}_{3}X
:

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}X

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{D}_{4}X-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{g}(X,\mathbf{D}_{4}e_{3})e_{4}-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{g}(X,\mathbf{D}_{4}e_{4})e_{3},

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}X

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{D}_{3}X-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{g}(X,\mathbf{D}_{3}e_{3})e_{4}-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{g}(X,\mathbf{D}_{3}e_{4})e_{3}.

A tensor field
\psi
defined on
\mathcal{M}
is called tangent to
S(t,u)
if it is a priori defined on the spacetime
\mathcal{M}
and all the possible contractions of
\psi
with either
e_{3}
or
e_{4}
are zero. We use
\not{\nabla}_{3}\psi
and
\not{\nabla}_{4}\psi
to denote the projection to
S(t,u)
of usual derivatives
\mathbf{D}_{3}\psi
and
\mathbf{D}_{4}\psi
.

As a direct consequence of (
2.10
), we have the Ricci formulae:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{D}_{A}e_{B}&=\not{\nabla}_{A}e_{B}+\frac{1}{2}\chi_{AB}e_{3}+\frac{1}{2}\underline{\chi}_{AB}e_{4},\\
\mathbf{D}_{A}e_{3}&=\underline{\chi}_{AB}e_{B}+\zeta_{A}e_{3},\\
\mathbf{D}_{A}e_{4}&=\chi_{AB}e_{B}-\zeta_{A}e_{4},\\
\mathbf{D}_{3}e_{A}&=\not{\nabla}_{3}e_{A}+\eta_{A}e_{3}+\underline{\xi}_{A}e_{4},\\
\mathbf{D}_{4}e_{A}&=\not{\nabla}_{4}e_{A}+\underline{\eta}_{A}e_{4}+\xi_{A}e_{4},\\
\mathbf{D}_{3}e_{3}&=-2\underline{\omega}e_{3}+2\underline{\xi}_{B}e_{B},\\
\mathbf{D}_{3}e_{4}&=2\underline{\omega}e_{4}+2\eta_{B}e_{B},\\
\mathbf{D}_{4}e_{4}&=-2\omega e_{4}+2\xi_{B}e_{B},\\
\mathbf{D}_{4}e_{3}&=2\omega e_{3}+2\underline{\eta}_{B}e_{B}.\end{split}

(2.12)

Next, we decompose
k
as follows:

\displaystyle\delta:=k_{NN},\qquad\epsilon_{A}:=k_{AN},\qquad\kappa_{AB}:=k_{AB}.

(2.13)

The following identities hold for a maximal-null foliation:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\chi_{AB}&=\theta_{AB}-\kappa_{AB},\qquad\qquad\,\,\underline{\chi}_{AB}=-\theta_{AB}-\kappa_{AB},\\
\xi_{A}&=0,\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\;\,\underline{\xi}_{A}=\not{\nabla}_{A}\log\phi-\not{\nabla}_{A}\log a,\\
\eta_{A}&=\not{\nabla}_{A}\log a+\epsilon_{A},\qquad\qquad\underline{\eta}_{A}=\not{\nabla}_{A}\log\phi-\epsilon_{A},\\
2\omega&=-\nabla_{N}\log\phi+\delta,\qquad\quad\;\;\,2\underline{\omega}=\nabla_{N}\log\phi+\delta,\\
\zeta_{A}&=\epsilon_{A},\end{split}

(2.14)

see (7.5.2b) in
[
5
]
.

Note that we have the following types of manifolds in this paper: the spacetime region
\mathcal{M}
, the spacelike hypersurfaces
\Sigma_{t}
, and the spheres
S(t,u)
. Every type of manifold has its metric, Levi-Civita connection and curvature tensor:

\displaystyle(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g},\mathbf{D},{\mathbf{R}}),\qquad(\Sigma_{t},g,\nabla,R),\qquad(S(t,u),\not{g},\not{\nabla},\not{R}).

2.2
Integral formulae

We define the
S
-average of scalar functions.

Definition 2.5
.

Given any scalar function
f
, we denote its average and its average free part by

\overline{f}:=\frac{1}{|S|}\int_{S}f\,d\not{g},\qquad\widecheck{f}:=f-\overline{f},

where
|S|
denotes the area of
S
.

The following lemma follows immediately from the definition.

Lemma 2.6
.

For any two scalar functions
u
and
v
, we have

\overline{uv}=\overline{u}\,\overline{v}+\overline{\widecheck{u}\widecheck{v}},

and

uv-\overline{uv}=\widecheck{u}\overline{v}+\overline{u}\widecheck{v}+\left(\widecheck{u}\widecheck{v}-\overline{\widecheck{u}\widecheck{v}}\right).

We recall the following integral formulae.

Lemma 2.7
.

For any scalar function
f
, the following equations hold:

\displaystyle\phi e_{4}\left(\int_{S(t,u)}fd\not{g}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{S(t,u)}\left(\phi e_{4}(f)+\phi\tr\chi f\right)d\not{g},

\displaystyle aN\left(\int_{S(t,u)}fd\not{g}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{S(t,u)}\left(aN(f)+a\tr\theta f\right)d\not{g}.

Taking
f=1
, we obtain

e_{4}(r)=\frac{\overline{\phi\tr\chi}}{2\phi}r,\qquad N(r)=\frac{\overline{a\tr\theta}}{2a}r,

(2.15)

where
r
is the
area radius
defined by

r(t,u):=\sqrt{\frac{|S(t,u)|}{4\pi}}.

Proof.

See Lemma 2.26 in
[
17
]
.
∎

2.3
Hodge systems in
2D
–geometry

Definition 2.8
.

For tensor fields defined on a
2
–sphere
S
, we denote by
\mathfrak{s}_{0}:=\mathfrak{s}_{0}(S)
the set of pairs of scalar functions,
\mathfrak{s}_{1}:=\mathfrak{s}_{1}(S)
the set of
1
–forms and
\mathfrak{s}_{2}:=\mathfrak{s}_{2}(S)
the set of symmetric traceless
2
–tensors.

Definition 2.9
.

Given
\xi\in\mathfrak{s}_{1}
, we define its Hodge dual
{}^{*}\xi
by

{{}^{*}\xi}_{A}:=\in_{AB}\xi^{B},

where
\in_{AB}
denotes the
volume element
on
S
. Clearly
{}^{*}\xi\in\mathfrak{s}_{1}
and

{}^{*}(^{*}\xi)=-\xi.

Given
U\in\mathfrak{s}_{2}
, we define its Hodge dual
{}^{*}U
by

{{}^{*}U}_{AB}:=\in_{AC}{U^{C}}_{B}.

Observe that
{}^{*}U\in\mathfrak{s}_{2}
and

{}^{*}(^{*}U)=-U.

Definition 2.10
.

Given
\xi,\eta\in\mathfrak{s}_{1}
, we denote

\displaystyle\xi\cdot\eta

\displaystyle:=\delta^{AB}\xi_{A}\eta_{B},

\displaystyle\xi\wedge\eta

\displaystyle:=\in^{AB}\xi_{A}\eta_{B},

\displaystyle(\xi\widehat{\otimes}\eta)_{AB}

\displaystyle:=\xi_{A}\eta_{B}+\xi_{B}\eta_{A}-\delta_{AB}\xi\cdot\eta.

Given
\xi\in\mathfrak{s}_{1}
,
U\in\mathfrak{s}_{2}
, we denote

\displaystyle(\xi\cdot U)_{A}:=\delta^{BC}\xi_{B}U_{AC}.

Given
U,V\in\mathfrak{s}_{2}
, we denote

\displaystyle(U\wedge V)_{AB}:=\in^{AB}U_{AC}V_{CB}.

Definition 2.11
.

For a given
\xi\in\mathfrak{s}_{1}
, we define the following differential operators:

\displaystyle{\not{\sdiv}\,}\xi

\displaystyle:=\delta^{AB}\not{\nabla}_{A}\xi_{B},

\displaystyle{\not{\curl}\,}\xi

\displaystyle:=\in^{AB}\not{\nabla}_{A}\xi_{B},

\displaystyle(\not{\nabla}\widehat{\otimes}\xi)_{AB}

\displaystyle:=\not{\nabla}_{A}\xi_{B}+\not{\nabla}_{B}\xi_{A}-\not{g}_{AB}({\not{\sdiv}\,}\xi).

Definition 2.12
.

We define the following Hodge type operators, as introduced in Section 2.2 in
[
5
]
:

•

\not{d}_{1}
takes
\mathfrak{s}_{1}
into pairs of
\mathfrak{s}_{0}
and is given by:

\not{d}_{1}\xi:=({\not{\sdiv}\,}\xi,{\not{\curl}\,}\xi),

•

\not{d}_{2}
takes
\mathfrak{s}_{2}
into
\mathfrak{s}_{1}
and is given by:

(\not{d}_{2}U)_{A}:=\not{\nabla}^{B}U_{AB},

•

\not{d}_{1}^{*}
takes pairs of
\mathfrak{s}_{0}
into
\mathfrak{s}_{1}
and is given by:

\displaystyle\not{d}_{1}^{*}(f,f_{*})_{A}:=-\not{\nabla}_{A}f+{\in_{A}}^{B}\not{\nabla}_{B}f_{*},

•

\not{d}_{2}^{*}
takes
\mathfrak{s}_{1}
into
\mathfrak{s}_{2}
and is given by:

\displaystyle\not{d}_{2}^{*}\xi:=-\frac{1}{2}\not{\nabla}\widehat{\otimes}\xi.

Proposition 2.13
.

We have the following identities:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\not{d}_{1}^{*}\not{d}_{1}&=-\not{\Delta}_{1}+\mathbf{K},\qquad\qquad\not{d}_{1}\not{d}_{1}^{*}=-\not{\Delta}_{{0}},\\
\not{d}_{2}^{*}\not{d}_{2}&=-\frac{1}{2}\not{\Delta}_{2}+\mathbf{K},\qquad\quad\;\not{d}_{2}\not{d}_{2}^{*}=-\frac{1}{2}(\not{\Delta}_{1}+\mathbf{K}),\end{split}

(2.16)

where
\mathbf{K}
denotes the Gauss curvature on
S
and
\not{\Delta}_{k}
denotes the Laplacian on
\mathfrak{s}_{k}
.

Proof.

See (2.2.2) in
[
5
]
.
∎

Definition 2.14
.

We define the weighted angular derivatives
\not{{\mathfrak{d}}}
as follows:

\displaystyle\not{{\mathfrak{d}}}U

\displaystyle:=r\not{d}_{2}U,\qquad\quad\;\;\quad\quad\,\,\forall U\in\mathfrak{s}_{2},

\displaystyle\not{{\mathfrak{d}}}\xi

\displaystyle:=r\not{d}_{1}\xi,\qquad\qquad\quad\quad\,\,\forall\xi\in\mathfrak{s}_{1},

\displaystyle\not{{\mathfrak{d}}}(f,f_{*})

\displaystyle:=r\not{d}_{1}^{*}(f,f_{*}),\qquad\forall(f,f_{*})\in\mathfrak{s}_{0}\times\mathfrak{s}_{0}.

We denote for any tensor
h\in\mathfrak{s}_{k}
,
k=0,1,2
,

h^{(0)}:=h,\qquad h^{(1)}:=(h,\not{{\mathfrak{d}}}h),\qquad h^{(2)}:=(h,\not{{\mathfrak{d}}}h,\not{{\mathfrak{d}}}^{2}h).

Proposition 2.15
.

We have the following commutator identities:

•

For any
U\in\mathfrak{s}_{2}

\displaystyle\not{{\mathfrak{d}}}\not{d}_{2}U=\not{d}_{1}\not{{\mathfrak{d}}}U.

•

For any
\xi\in\mathfrak{s}_{1}

\displaystyle\not{{\mathfrak{d}}}\not{d}_{1}\xi

\displaystyle=\not{d}_{1}^{*}\not{{\mathfrak{d}}}\xi,

\displaystyle\not{{\mathfrak{d}}}(2\not{d}_{2}^{*}\xi)

\displaystyle=\not{d}_{1}^{*}\not{{\mathfrak{d}}}\xi-2r\mathbf{K}\xi.

•

For any
(f,f_{*})\in\mathfrak{s}_{0}

\displaystyle\not{{\mathfrak{d}}}\not{d}_{1}^{*}(f,f_{*})=\not{d}_{1}\not{{\mathfrak{d}}}(f,f_{*}).

Proof.

We recall from (
2.16
) that for any
\xi\in\mathfrak{s}_{1}

\displaystyle 2\not{{\mathfrak{d}}}\not{d}_{2}^{*}\xi=2r\not{d}_{2}\not{d}_{2}^{*}\xi=-r(\not{\Delta}_{1}+\mathbf{K})\xi=r(\not{d}_{1}^{*}\not{d}_{1}-2\mathbf{K})\xi=\not{d}_{1}^{*}\not{{\mathfrak{d}}}\xi-2r\mathbf{K}\xi,

which implies the third identity. The other identities follow directly from Definition
2.14
. This concludes the proof of Proposition
2.15
.
∎

Definition 2.16
.

For a tensor field
f
on a
2
–sphere
S
, we denote its
L^{p}
–norm as follows:

|f|_{p,S}:=\left(\int_{S}|f|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

(2.17)

2.4
Null structure equations

We recall the null structure equations, see Proposition 7.4.1 in
[
5
]
.

Proposition 2.17
.

We have the null structure equations:

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\widehat{\chi}+\tr\chi\,\widehat{\chi}

\displaystyle=\not{\nabla}\widehat{\otimes}\xi-2\omega\widehat{\chi}+(\eta+\underline{\eta}+2\zeta)\widehat{\otimes}\xi-\alpha,

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\tr\chi+\frac{1}{2}(\tr\chi)^{2}

\displaystyle=2{\not{\sdiv}\,}\xi-2\omega\tr\chi+2\xi\cdot(\eta+\underline{\eta}+2\zeta)-|\widehat{\chi}|^{2},

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\widehat{\underline{\chi}}+\tr\underline{\chi}\,\widehat{\underline{\chi}}

\displaystyle=\not{\nabla}\widehat{\otimes}\underline{\xi}-2\underline{\omega}\widehat{\underline{\chi}}+(\eta+\underline{\eta}-2\zeta)\widehat{\otimes}\underline{\xi}-\underline{\alpha},

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\tr\underline{\chi}+\frac{1}{2}(\tr\underline{\chi})^{2}

\displaystyle=2{\not{\sdiv}\,}\underline{\xi}-2\underline{\omega}\tr\underline{\chi}+2\underline{\xi}\cdot(\eta+\underline{\eta}-2\zeta)-|\widehat{\underline{\chi}}|^{2},

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\widehat{\underline{\chi}}+\frac{1}{2}\tr\chi\,\widehat{\underline{\chi}}

\displaystyle=\not{\nabla}\widehat{\otimes}\underline{\eta}+2\omega\widehat{\underline{\chi}}-\frac{1}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}\,\widehat{\chi}+\underline{\xi}\widehat{\otimes}\underline{\xi}+\underline{\eta}\widehat{\otimes}\underline{\eta},

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\tr\underline{\chi}+\frac{1}{2}\tr\chi\tr\underline{\chi}

\displaystyle=2\omega\tr\underline{\chi}-\widehat{\chi}\cdot\widehat{\underline{\chi}}+2{\not{\sdiv}\,}\underline{\eta}+2(\underline{\xi}\cdot\xi+|\underline{\eta}|^{2})+2\rho,

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\widehat{\chi}+\frac{1}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}\,\widehat{\chi}

\displaystyle=\not{\nabla}\widehat{\otimes}\eta+2\underline{\omega}\widehat{\chi}-\frac{1}{2}\tr\chi\,\widehat{\underline{\chi}}+\xi\widehat{\otimes}\xi+\eta\widehat{\otimes}\eta,

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\tr\chi+\frac{1}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}\tr\chi

\displaystyle=2\underline{\omega}\tr\chi-\widehat{\chi}\cdot\widehat{\underline{\chi}}+2{\not{\sdiv}\,}\eta+2(\xi\cdot\underline{\xi}+|\eta|^{2})+2\rho,

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\eta-\not{\nabla}_{3}\xi

\displaystyle=-4\underline{\omega}\xi-\chi\cdot(\eta-\underline{\eta})-\beta,

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\underline{\eta}-\not{\nabla}_{4}\underline{\xi}

\displaystyle=-4\omega\underline{\xi}-\underline{\chi}\cdot(\underline{\eta}-\eta)+\underline{\beta},

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\zeta

\displaystyle=-2\not{\nabla}\underline{\omega}-\underline{\chi}\cdot(\zeta+\eta)+2\underline{\omega}(\zeta-\eta)+\chi\cdot\underline{\xi}+2\omega\underline{\xi}-\underline{\beta},

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\zeta

\displaystyle=2\not{\nabla}\omega+\chi\cdot(-\zeta+\underline{\eta})+2\omega(\zeta+\underline{\eta})-\underline{\chi}\cdot\xi-2\underline{\omega}\xi-\beta,

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\omega+\not{\nabla}_{4}\underline{\omega}

\displaystyle=\xi\cdot\underline{\xi}+\zeta\cdot(\eta-\underline{\eta})-\eta\cdot\underline{\eta}+4\omega\underline{\omega}+\rho.

Also, we have the Codazzi equations:

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\not{\sdiv}\,}\widehat{\chi}=\frac{1}{2}\not{\nabla}\tr\chi-\zeta\cdot\left(\widehat{\chi}-\frac{1}{2}\tr\chi\right)-\beta,\\
{\not{\sdiv}\,}\widehat{\underline{\chi}}=\frac{1}{2}\not{\nabla}\tr\underline{\chi}+\zeta\cdot\left(\widehat{\underline{\chi}}-\frac{1}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}\right)+\underline{\beta},\end{split}

(2.18)

the torsion equation:

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\not{\curl}\,}\eta&=\sigma+\frac{1}{2}\widehat{}\underline{\chi}\wedge\widehat{\chi}-\xi\wedge\underline{\xi},\\
{\not{\curl}\,}\underline{\eta}&=-\sigma-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{}\underline{\chi}\wedge\widehat{\chi}+\xi\wedge\underline{\xi},\\
{\not{\curl}\,}\xi&=\xi\wedge(\eta+\underline{\eta}+2\zeta),\\
{\not{\curl}\,}\underline{\xi}&=\underline{\xi}\wedge(\eta+\underline{\eta}-2\zeta),\end{split}

(2.19)

and the Gauss equation:

\mathbf{K}=-\frac{1}{4}\tr\chi\tr\underline{\chi}+\frac{1}{2}\widehat{}\underline{\chi}\cdot\widehat{\chi}-\rho.

(2.20)

2.5
Bianchi equations

We recall the Bianchi equations, see Proposition 7.3.2 in
[
5
]
.

Proposition 2.18
.

The Bianchi equations take the following form

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}\,\alpha

\displaystyle=\not{\nabla}\widehat{\otimes}\beta+4\underline{\omega}\alpha-3(\widehat{\chi}\rho+{{}^{*}\widehat{\chi}}\sigma)+(\zeta+4\eta)\widehat{\otimes}\beta,

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\beta+2\tr\chi\,\beta

\displaystyle={\not{\sdiv}\,}\alpha-2\omega\beta+(2\zeta+\underline{\eta})\cdot\alpha+3(\xi\rho+{{}^{*}\xi}\sigma),

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\beta+\tr\underline{\chi}\,\beta

\displaystyle=\not{\nabla}\rho+{{}^{*}\not{\nabla}}\sigma+2\underline{\omega}\beta+\underline{\xi}\cdot\alpha+2\widehat{\chi}\cdot\underline{\beta}+3(\eta\rho+{{}^{*}\eta}\sigma),

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\rho+\frac{3}{2}\tr\chi\,\rho

\displaystyle={\not{\sdiv}\,}\beta-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\underline{\chi}}\cdot\alpha+\zeta\cdot\beta+2(\underline{\eta}\cdot\beta-\xi\cdot\underline{\beta}),

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\rho+\frac{3}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}\,\rho

\displaystyle=-{\not{\sdiv}\,}\underline{\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\chi}\cdot\underline{\alpha}+\zeta\cdot\underline{\beta}+2(\underline{\xi}\cdot\beta-\eta\cdot\underline{\beta}),

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\sigma+\frac{3}{2}\tr\chi\,\sigma

\displaystyle=-{\not{\curl}\,}\beta+\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\underline{\chi}}\cdot{{}^{*}\alpha}-\zeta\cdot{{}^{*}\beta}-2(\underline{\eta}\cdot{{}^{*}\beta}+2\xi\cdot{{}^{*}\underline{\beta}}),

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\sigma+\frac{3}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}\,\sigma

\displaystyle=-{\not{\curl}\,}\underline{\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\chi}\cdot{{}^{*}\underline{\alpha}}+\zeta\cdot{{}^{*}\underline{\beta}}-2(\eta\cdot{{}^{*}\underline{\beta}}+\underline{\xi}\cdot{{}^{*}\beta}),

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\underline{\beta}+\tr\chi\,\underline{\beta}

\displaystyle=-\not{\nabla}\rho+{{}^{*}\not{\nabla}\sigma}+2\omega\underline{\beta}+2\widehat{\underline{\chi}}\cdot\beta-\xi\cdot\underline{\alpha}-3(\underline{\eta}\rho-{{}^{*}\underline{\eta}}\sigma),

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\underline{\beta}+2\tr\underline{\chi}\,\underline{\beta}

\displaystyle=-{\not{\sdiv}\,}\underline{\alpha}-2\underline{\omega}\underline{\beta}+(2\zeta-\eta)\cdot\underline{\alpha}+3(-\underline{\xi}\rho+{{}^{*}\underline{\xi}}\sigma),

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\underline{\alpha}+\frac{1}{2}\tr\chi\,\underline{\alpha}

\displaystyle=-\not{\nabla}\widehat{\otimes}\underline{\beta}+4\omega\underline{\alpha}-3(\widehat{\underline{\chi}}\rho-{{}^{*}\widehat{\underline{\chi}}}\sigma)+(\zeta-4\underline{\eta})\widehat{\otimes}\underline{\beta}.

2.6
Commutation identities

We recall the following commutation lemma.

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
U_{A_{1}...A_{k}}
be an
S
-tangent
k
-covariant tensor on
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
. Then

\displaystyle[\not{\nabla}_{4},\not{\nabla}_{B}]U_{A_{1}...A_{k}}

\displaystyle=-\chi_{BC}\not{\nabla}_{C}U_{A_{1}...A_{k}}+F_{4BA_{1}...A_{k}},

\displaystyle F_{4BA_{1}...A_{k}}

\displaystyle:=\xi_{B}\not{\nabla}_{3}U_{A_{1},...,A_{k}}+(\zeta_{B}+\underline{\eta}_{B})\not{\nabla}_{4}U_{A_{1}...A_{k}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\underline{\chi}_{A_{i}B}\,\xi_{C}-\underline{\chi}_{BC}\,\xi_{A_{i}}+\chi_{A_{i}B}\,\underline{\eta}_{C}-\chi_{BC}\,\underline{\eta}_{A_{i}}+\in_{A_{i}C}{{}^{*}\beta}_{B})U_{A_{1}...C...A_{k}},

\displaystyle[\not{\nabla}_{3},\not{\nabla}_{B}]U_{A_{1}...A_{k}}

\displaystyle=-\underline{\chi}_{BC}\not{\nabla}_{C}U_{A_{1}...A_{k}}+F_{3BA_{1}...A_{k}},

\displaystyle F_{3BA_{1}...A_{k}}

\displaystyle:=\underline{\xi}_{B}\not{\nabla}_{4}U_{A_{1},...,A_{k}}+(\eta_{B}-\zeta_{B})\not{\nabla}_{3}U_{A_{1}...A_{k}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\chi_{A_{i}B}\underline{\xi}_{C}-\chi_{BC}\underline{\xi}_{A_{i}}+\underline{\chi}_{A_{i}B}\,\eta_{C}-\underline{\chi}_{BC}\,\eta_{A_{i}}+\in_{A_{i}C}{{}^{*}\underline{\beta}}_{B})U_{A_{1}...C...A_{k}},

\displaystyle[\not{\nabla}_{3},\not{\nabla}_{4}]U_{A_{1}...A_{k}}

\displaystyle=F_{34A_{1}...A_{k}},

\displaystyle F_{34A_{1}...A_{k}}

\displaystyle:=-2\omega\not{\nabla}_{3}U+2\underline{\omega}\not{\nabla}_{4}U+(\eta_{B}-\underline{\eta}_{B})\not{\nabla}_{B}U_{A_{1}...A_{k}}

\displaystyle+2\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\xi_{A_{i}}\,\underline{\xi}_{C}-\xi_{C}\,\underline{\xi}_{A_{i}}+\underline{\eta}_{A_{i}}\,\eta_{C}-\underline{\eta}_{A_{i}}\,\eta_{C}+\in_{A_{i}C}\sigma)U_{A_{1}...C...A_{k}}.

Proof.

See Lemma 7.3.3 in
[
5
]
.
∎

2.7
The electric-magnetic decomposition of curvature

Proposition 2.20
.

We define the electric-magnetic decomposition of
{\mathbf{R}}
:

\displaystyle E_{ij}:={\mathbf{R}}(T,e_{i},T,e_{j}),\qquad H_{ij}:={{}^{*}{\mathbf{R}}}(T,e_{i},T,e_{j}),\qquad i,j=1,2,3.

(2.21)

Then, the following identities hold for a maximal foliation:

\displaystyle\begin{split}E_{AB}&=\frac{1}{4}\alpha_{AB}+\frac{1}{4}\underline{\alpha}_{AB}-\frac{1}{2}\rho\,\delta_{AB},\qquad\;H_{AB}=-\frac{1}{4}{{}^{*}\alpha}_{AB}+\frac{1}{4}{{}^{*}\underline{\alpha}}_{AB}-\frac{1}{2}\sigma\,\delta_{AB},\\
E_{AN}&=\frac{1}{2}\underline{\beta}_{A}+\frac{1}{2}\beta_{A},\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\;\;\;H_{AN}=\frac{1}{2}{{}^{*}\underline{\beta}}_{A}-\frac{1}{2}{{}^{*}\beta}_{A},\\
E_{NN}&=\rho,\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\;H_{NN}=\sigma.\end{split}

(2.22)

Proof.

See (7.3.3e) in
[
5
]
.
∎

Proposition 2.21
.

The following equations hold for a maximal foliation:

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}k_{jm}-\nabla_{j}k_{im}

\displaystyle={\in_{ij}}^{l}H_{lm},

\displaystyle\sRic_{ij}-k_{il}{k^{l}}_{j}

\displaystyle=E_{ij}.

Proof.

See (1.0.8a)–(1.0.8b) in
[
5
]
.
∎

Definition 2.22
.

Let
\Sigma
be a
3
–dimensional manifold diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We define the following differential operators on
\Sigma
:

•

For any scalar function
\phi
on
\Sigma
, we define

\displaystyle\Delta\phi=\nabla^{i}\nabla_{i}\phi.

•

For any
1
–form
\xi
on
\Sigma
, we define

\displaystyle\sdiv\xi

\displaystyle:=\nabla^{i}\xi_{i},

\displaystyle(\curl\xi)_{i}

\displaystyle:={\in_{i}}^{lm}\nabla_{l}\xi_{m}.

•

For any traceless symmetric
2
–tensor
U
on
\Sigma
, we define

\displaystyle(\sdiv U)_{i}

\displaystyle:=\nabla^{j}U_{ij},

\displaystyle(\curl U)_{ij}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\left({\in_{i}}^{lm}\nabla_{l}U_{mj}+{\in_{j}}^{lm}\nabla_{l}U_{mi}\right).

Proposition 2.23
.

Let
E
,
H
be the electric-magnetic decomposition defined in (
2.21
). Then, the following identities hold:

\displaystyle\sdiv E

\displaystyle=k\wedge H,

\displaystyle\sdiv H

\displaystyle=-k\wedge E,

\displaystyle-\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{T}H+\curl E

\displaystyle=-\nabla\log\phi\wedge E-\frac{1}{2}k\times H,

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{T}E+\curl H

\displaystyle=-\nabla\log\phi\wedge H+\frac{1}{2}k\times E,

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{T}E)_{ij}&:=(\mathcal{L}_{T}E)_{ij}-\frac{2}{3}(k\cdot E)g_{ij},\\
(\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{T}H)_{ij}&:=(\mathcal{L}_{T}H)_{ij}-\frac{2}{3}(k\cdot H)g_{ij},\end{split}

(2.23)

and where
\mathcal{L}_{X}U
denotes the Lie derivative of a tensor
U
relative to a vectorfield
X
.

Proof.

See Proposition 7.2.1 in
[
5
]
.
∎

3
Main theorem

3.1
Preliminary definitions

Definition 3.1
.

For any
t\geq 2
and
-\infty\leq u_{1}<u_{2}\leq+\infty
, we denote

\displaystyle V_{t}(u_{1},u_{2}):=J^{+}(\Sigma_{2})\cap J^{-}(\Sigma_{t})\cap\{u_{1}\leq u\leq u_{2}\},

and for
V=V_{t}(u_{1},u_{2})
and
u_{1}\leq u\leq u_{2}

\displaystyle{C_{u}^{V}}:=C_{u}\cap V,\qquad\qquad\quad{\Sigma_{t}^{V}}:=\Sigma_{t}\cap V.

We then define for any tensorfield
\Psi
:

\displaystyle B_{p}^{q}[\Psi](u_{1},u_{2})

\displaystyle:=\int_{V}r^{p-1}|\Psi^{(q)}|^{2},

\displaystyle E_{p}^{q}[\Psi](u)

\displaystyle:=\int_{{C_{u}^{V}}}r^{p}|\Psi^{(q)}|^{2},

\displaystyle F_{p}^{q}[\Psi](u_{1},u_{2})

\displaystyle:=\int_{{\Sigma_{t}^{V}}}r^{p}|\Psi^{(q)}|^{2},

\displaystyle D_{\ell}^{q}[\Psi](u)

\displaystyle:=\sup_{S\subseteq{C_{u}^{V}}}\sup_{p\in[2,4]}|r^{\frac{\ell+3}{2}-\frac{2}{p}}\Psi^{(q)}|_{p,S}^{2},

\displaystyle D^{(\infty)}_{\ell}[\Psi](u)

\displaystyle:=\sup_{{C_{u}^{V}}}|r^{\frac{\ell+3}{2}}\Psi|^{2},

where
q=0,1,2,3
and where the notation
\Psi^{(q)}
has been introduced in Definition
2.14
. We also use the following shorthand notations:

\displaystyle V_{t}(u):=V_{t}(-\infty,u),\qquad X_{p}^{q}[\Psi](u):=X_{p}^{q}[\Psi](-\infty,u),\quad\mbox{ where }\;X=B,F.

We also denote

\displaystyle X_{p}[\Psi](u_{1},u_{2}):=X_{p}^{0}[\Psi](u_{1},u_{2}),\quad\mbox{ where }\;X=B,E,F,D.

Moreover, we denote

\displaystyle XY_{p}^{q}[\Psi](u_{1},u_{2}):=X_{p}^{q}[\Psi](u_{1},u_{2})+Y_{p}^{q}[\Psi](u_{1},u_{2})\quad\mbox{ where }\;X,Y\in\{B,E,F\},

and

E_{p}^{q}[\Psi](u_{1},u_{2}):=\sup_{u\in[u_{1},u_{2}]}E_{p}^{q}[\Psi](u).

Definition 3.2
.

Let
2<t_{*}<+\infty
and
q=0,1,2,3
. We define:

\displaystyle{{}^{e}\mathcal{B}}_{p,d}^{q}[\Psi]

\displaystyle:=\sup_{2\leq t\leq t_{*}}\sup_{u\leq 1}{\langle u\rangle}^{d}B_{p}^{q}[\Psi](u),

\displaystyle{{}^{e}\mathcal{E}}_{p,d}^{q}[\Psi]

\displaystyle:=\sup_{2\leq t\leq t_{*}}\sup_{u\leq 1}{\langle u\rangle}^{d}E_{p}^{q}[\Psi](u),

\displaystyle{{}^{e}\mathcal{F}}_{p,d}^{q}[\Psi]

\displaystyle:=\sup_{2\leq t\leq t_{*}}\sup_{u\leq 1}{\langle u\rangle}^{d}F_{p}^{q}[\Psi](u),

\displaystyle{{}^{e}\mathcal{D}}_{p,d}^{q}[\Psi]

\displaystyle:=\sup_{2\leq t\leq t_{*}}\sup_{u\leq 1}{\langle u\rangle}^{d}D_{p}^{q}[\Psi](u),

{}^{e}\mathcal{D}^{(\infty)}_{p,d}[\Psi]

\displaystyle:=\sup_{2\leq t\leq t_{*}}\sup_{u\leq 1}{\langle u\rangle}^{d}D^{(\infty)}_{p}[\Psi](u),

where

{\langle u\rangle}:=\sqrt{1+|u|^{2}}.

(3.1)

We also define:

\displaystyle{{}^{i}\mathcal{B}}_{p,d}^{q}[\Psi]

\displaystyle:=\sup_{2\leq t\leq t_{*}}\sup_{1\leq u_{1}\leq u_{2}\leq u_{c}(t)}u_{1}^{d}B_{p}^{q}[\Psi](u_{1},u_{2}),

\displaystyle{{}^{i}\mathcal{E}}_{p,d}^{q}[\Psi]

\displaystyle:=\sup_{2\leq t\leq t_{*}}\sup_{1\leq u\leq u_{c}(t)}u^{d}E_{p}^{q}[\Psi](u),

\displaystyle{{}^{i}\mathcal{F}}_{p,d}^{q}[\Psi]

\displaystyle:=\sup_{2\leq t\leq t_{*}}\sup_{1\leq u_{1}\leq u_{2}\leq u_{c}(t)}u_{1}^{d}F_{p}^{q}[\Psi](u_{1},u_{2}),

\displaystyle{{}^{i}\mathcal{D}}_{p,d}^{q}[\Psi]

\displaystyle:=\sup_{2\leq t\leq t_{*}}\sup_{1\leq u\leq u_{c}(t)}u^{d}D_{p}^{q}[\Psi](u),

{}^{i}\mathcal{D}^{(\infty)}_{p,d}[\Psi]

\displaystyle:=\sup_{2\leq t\leq t_{*}}\sup_{1\leq u\leq u_{c}(t)}u^{d}D^{(\infty)}_{p,d}[\Psi](u).

Moreover, we denote

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{p,d}^{q}[\Psi]:={{}^{e}\mathcal{X}}_{p,d}^{q}[\Psi]+{{}^{i}\mathcal{X}}_{p,d}^{q}[\Psi],\quad\mbox{ where }\;\mathcal{X}\in\{\mathcal{B},\,\mathcal{E},\,\mathcal{F},\,\mathcal{D}\}.

We also use the following shorthand notations:

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{p,d}[\Psi]:=\mathcal{X}_{p,d}^{0}[\Psi],\qquad\mathcal{X}_{p}^{q}[\Psi]:=\mathcal{X}_{p,0}^{q}[\Psi],\qquad\mathcal{X}_{p}[\Psi]:=\mathcal{X}_{p,0}^{0}[\Psi],\quad\mbox{ where }\;\mathcal{X}\in\{\mathcal{B},\,\mathcal{E},\,\mathcal{F},\,\mathcal{D}\}.

Definition 3.3
.

We define the renormalized quantity
{\not{U}}
and mass aspect function
\mu
:
7
7

7

The quantities
{\not{U}}
and
\mu
appeared first in
[
5
]
.

{\not{U}}:=\not{\nabla}\tr\chi+\tr\chi\,\zeta,\qquad\mu:=-{\not{\sdiv}\,}\eta-\rho+\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\chi}\cdot\widehat{\underline{\chi}}.

(3.2)

We also define the following renormalized curvature components:
8
8

8

This renormalization appeared first in
[
17
]
.

\wideparen{\rho}:=\rho-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\chi}\cdot\widehat{\underline{\chi}},\qquad\wideparen{\sigma}:=\sigma-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\chi}\wedge\widehat{\underline{\chi}}.

(3.3)

Remark 3.4
.

The purpose of defining the renormalized quantities
\wideparen{\rho}
and
\wideparen{\sigma}
is to get rid of the terms
\widehat{\chi}\cdot\underline{\alpha}
and
\widehat{\chi}\wedge\underline{\alpha}
in the right hand side of the Bianchi equations for
\not{\nabla}_{3}\rho
and
\not{\nabla}_{3}\sigma
, as they are the most dangerous among all the nonlinear terms appearing in the Bianchi equations. See Lemma
4.9
for the Bianchi equations satisfied by
(\wideparen{\rho},\wideparen{\sigma})
. Note that the terms
\widehat{\chi}\cdot\underline{\alpha}
and
\widehat{\chi}\wedge\underline{\alpha}
are emphasized as being borderline for the case
s=2
in
[
1
]
.

3.2
Main norms

The norms in Sections
3.2.1
–
3.2.3
are defined in the bootstrap region
V_{t_{*}}
while the norms in Section
3.2.4
are defined in the initial layer region
\mathcal{V}_{(0)}
. Also, throughout Sections
3.2.1
–
3.2.3
, the norms
{{}^{E}\mathcal{R}}
,
{{}^{E}\mathcal{K}}
and
{{}^{E}\mathcal{O}}
are defined in the whole region
V_{t_{*}}={{}^{e}V}\cup{{}^{i}V}
while the norms
{{}^{I}\mathcal{R}}
,
{{}^{T}\mathcal{R}}
,
{{}^{I}\mathcal{K}}
,
{{}^{T}\mathcal{K}}
,
{{}^{I}\mathcal{O}}
and
{{}^{T}\mathcal{O}}
are defined in the interior region
{{}^{i}V}
only.

3.2.1
\mathcal{R}
norms (curvature components)

We define

\displaystyle{{}^{E}\mathcal{R}}:={{}^{E}\mathcal{R}}[\alpha]+{{}^{E}\mathcal{R}}[\beta]+{{}^{E}\mathcal{R}}[\wideparen{\rho},\wideparen{\sigma}]+{{}^{E}\mathcal{R}}[\underline{\beta}]+{{}^{E}\mathcal{R}}[\underline{\alpha}]+{{}^{E}\mathcal{R}}[\alpha_{4}]+{{}^{E}\mathcal{R}}[\underline{\alpha}_{3}],

where

\displaystyle\big({{}^{E}\mathcal{R}}[\alpha]\big)^{2}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{B}_{s}^{1}[\alpha]+\mathcal{E}_{s}^{1}[\alpha]+\mathcal{F}_{s}^{1}[\alpha]+\mathcal{D}_{s}[\alpha],

\displaystyle\big({{}^{E}\mathcal{R}}[\beta]\big)^{2}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{B}_{s}^{1}[\beta]+\mathcal{E}_{s}^{1}[\beta]+\mathcal{F}_{s}^{1}[\beta]+\mathcal{D}_{s}[\beta],

\displaystyle\big({{}^{E}\mathcal{R}}[\wideparen{\rho},\wideparen{\sigma}]\big)^{2}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{B}_{s}^{1}[\wideparen{\rho},\wideparen{\sigma}]+\mathcal{E}_{s}^{1}[\wideparen{\rho},\wideparen{\sigma}]+\mathcal{F}_{s}^{1}[\wideparen{\rho},\wideparen{\sigma}]+\mathcal{D}_{s}[\wideparen{\rho},\wideparen{\sigma}],

\displaystyle\big({{}^{E}\mathcal{R}}[\underline{\beta}]\big)^{2}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{E}_{0,s}^{1}[\underline{\beta}]+\mathcal{F}_{s}^{1}[\underline{\beta}]+\mathcal{D}_{s-1,1}[\underline{\beta}],

\displaystyle\big({{}^{E}\mathcal{R}}[\underline{\alpha}]\big)^{2}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{F}_{0,s}^{1}[\underline{\alpha}]+\mathcal{D}_{-1,s+1}[\underline{\alpha}],

\displaystyle\big({{}^{E}\mathcal{R}}[\alpha_{4}]\big)^{2}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{B}_{s}[\alpha_{4}]+\mathcal{E}_{s}[\alpha_{4}]+\mathcal{F}_{s}[\alpha_{4}],

\displaystyle\big({{}^{E}\mathcal{R}}[\underline{\alpha}_{3}]\big)^{2}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{F}_{-2,s+2}[\underline{\alpha}_{3}],

with

\alpha_{4}:=r^{-4}\not{\nabla}_{4}(r^{5}\alpha),\qquad\underline{\alpha}_{3}:=r^{-4}\not{\nabla}_{3}(r^{5}\underline{\alpha}).

Next, we define

\displaystyle{{}^{I}\mathcal{R}}:={{}^{I}\mathcal{R}}[\alpha]+{{}^{I}\mathcal{R}}[\beta]+{{}^{I}\mathcal{R}}[\wideparen{\rho},\wideparen{\sigma}]+{{}^{I}\mathcal{R}}[\underline{\beta}]+{{}^{I}\mathcal{R}}[\underline{\alpha}]+{{}^{I}\mathcal{R}}[\alpha_{4}],

where

\displaystyle\big({{}^{I}\mathcal{R}}[R]\big)^{2}

\displaystyle:={{}^{i}\mathcal{E}}^{1}_{0,s}[R]+{{}^{i}\mathcal{F}}_{-2,s+2}^{1}[R]+{{}^{i}\mathcal{D}}_{-2,s+2}[R],\qquad\forall\;R\in\{\alpha,\beta,\wideparen{\rho},\wideparen{\sigma},\underline{\beta},\underline{\alpha}\},

\displaystyle\big({{}^{I}\mathcal{R}}[\alpha_{4}]\big)^{2}

\displaystyle:={{}^{i}\mathcal{E}}_{0,s}[\alpha_{4}]+{{}^{i}\mathcal{F}}_{-2,s+2}[\alpha_{4}]+{{}^{i}\mathcal{D}}_{-2,s+2}[\alpha_{4}].

We also define the following time derivative norms:

\displaystyle{{}^{T}\mathcal{R}}:={{}^{T}\mathcal{R}}[\alpha]+{{}^{T}\mathcal{R}}[\beta]+{{}^{T}\mathcal{R}}[\rho,\sigma]+{{}^{T}\mathcal{R}}[\underline{\beta}]+{{}^{T}\mathcal{R}}[\underline{\alpha}],

where

\displaystyle\big({{}^{T}\mathcal{R}}[R]\big)^{2}

\displaystyle:={{}^{i}\mathcal{E}}_{0,s+2}[\not{\nabla}_{T}R]+{{}^{i}\mathcal{F}}_{0,s+2}[\not{\nabla}_{T}R],\qquad\forall\;R\in\{\alpha,\beta,\rho,\sigma,\underline{\beta}\},

\displaystyle\big({{}^{T}\mathcal{R}}[\underline{\alpha}]\big)^{2}

\displaystyle:={{}^{i}\mathcal{F}}_{0,s+2}[\not{\nabla}_{T}\underline{\alpha}].

Finally, we denote

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}:={{}^{E}\mathcal{R}}+{{}^{I}\mathcal{R}}+{{}^{T}\mathcal{R}}.

3.2.2
\mathcal{K}
norms (maximal connection coefficients)

We define

\displaystyle{{}^{E}\mathcal{K}}:={{}^{E}\mathcal{K}}[\delta]+{{}^{E}\mathcal{K}}[\epsilon]+{{}^{E}\mathcal{K}}[\widehat{\kappa}]+{{}^{E}\mathcal{K}}[\phi],

where

\displaystyle\big({{}^{E}\mathcal{K}}[\delta]\big)^{2}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{F}_{s-2}^{2}[\delta]+\mathcal{F}_{s}^{1}[\not{\nabla}_{N}\delta]+\mathcal{D}_{s-2}^{1}[\delta]+\mathcal{D}_{s-1,1}[\not{\nabla}_{N}\delta],

\displaystyle\big({{}^{E}\mathcal{K}}[\epsilon]\big)^{2}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{F}_{s-2}^{2}[\epsilon]+\mathcal{F}_{s}^{1}[\not{\nabla}_{N}\epsilon]+\mathcal{D}_{s-2}^{1}[\epsilon]+\mathcal{D}_{s-1,1}[\not{\nabla}_{N}\epsilon],

\displaystyle\big({{}^{E}\mathcal{K}}[\widehat{\kappa}]\big)^{2}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{F}_{s-2}^{2}[\widehat{\kappa}]+\mathcal{F}_{s}^{1}[\not{\nabla}_{N}\widehat{\kappa}]+\mathcal{D}^{1}_{s-3,1}[\widehat{\kappa}]+\mathcal{D}_{-1,s-1}[\widehat{\kappa}]+\mathcal{D}_{-1,s+1}[\not{\nabla}_{N}\widehat{\kappa}],

\displaystyle\big({{}^{E}\mathcal{K}}[\phi]\big)^{2}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{F}_{s-4}^{3}[\log\phi]+\mathcal{F}_{s-2}^{2}[\nabla_{N}\phi]+\mathcal{F}_{s}^{1}[\nabla_{N}\nabla_{N}\phi]

\displaystyle\;+\mathcal{D}_{s-4}^{2}[\log\phi]+\mathcal{D}_{s-2}^{1}[\nabla_{N}\phi]+\mathcal{D}_{s-1,1}[\nabla_{N}\nabla_{N}\phi].

Next, we define

\displaystyle{{}^{I}\mathcal{K}}:={{}^{I}\mathcal{K}}[k]+{{}^{I}\mathcal{K}}[\phi],

where

\displaystyle\big({{}^{I}\mathcal{K}}[k]\big)^{2}:=

\displaystyle{{}^{i}\mathcal{F}}_{-2,s}[k]+{{}^{i}\mathcal{F}}_{0,s}[\nabla k]+{{}^{i}\mathcal{F}}_{0,s+2}[\nabla^{2}k]+{{}^{i}D^{(\infty)}_{-3,s+1}}[k]+{{}^{i}\mathcal{D}}_{-2,s+2}[\nabla k],

\displaystyle\big({{}^{I}\mathcal{K}}[\phi]\big)^{2}:=

\displaystyle{{}^{i}\mathcal{F}}_{-3,s-1}[\log\phi]+{{}^{i}\mathcal{F}}_{-2,s}[\nabla\phi]+{{}^{i}\mathcal{F}}_{0,s}[\nabla^{2}\phi]+{{}^{i}\mathcal{F}}_{0,s+2}[\nabla^{3}\phi]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle{{}^{i}D^{(\infty)}_{-3,s-1}}[\log\phi]+{{}^{i}D^{(\infty)}_{-3,s+1}}[\nabla\phi]+{{}^{i}\mathcal{D}}_{-2,s+2}[\nabla^{2}\phi].

We also define the following time derivative norms:

\displaystyle{{}^{T}\mathcal{K}}:={{}^{T}\mathcal{K}}[\delta]+{{}^{T}\mathcal{K}}[\epsilon]+{{}^{T}\mathcal{K}}[\widehat{\kappa}]+{{}^{T}\mathcal{K}}[\phi],

where

\displaystyle\big({{}^{T}\mathcal{K}}[\delta]\big)^{2}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{p\in[-2,s-1]}{{}^{i}\mathcal{D}}_{p,s-p}[\not{\nabla}_{T}\delta],

\displaystyle\big({{}^{T}\mathcal{K}}[\epsilon]\big)^{2}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{p\in[-2,s-1]}{{}^{i}\mathcal{D}}_{p,s-p}[\not{\nabla}_{T}\epsilon],

\displaystyle\big({{}^{T}\mathcal{K}}[\widehat{\kappa}]\big)^{2}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{p\in[-2,-1]}{{}^{i}\mathcal{D}}_{p,s-p}[\not{\nabla}_{T}\widehat{\kappa}],

\displaystyle\big({{}^{T}\mathcal{K}}[\phi]\big)^{2}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{p\in[-2,s-1]}{{}^{i}\mathcal{D}}_{p,s-p}[\not{\nabla}_{T}\nabla\phi].

Finally, we denote

\mathcal{K}:={{}^{E}\mathcal{K}}+{{}^{I}\mathcal{K}}+{{}^{T}\mathcal{K}}.

3.2.3
\mathcal{O}
norms (null connection coefficients)

In the rest of this paper, we always denote by
{\delta_{B}}
a fixed constant satisfying

\displaystyle 0<{\delta_{B}}\ll s-1.

(3.4)

We define

\displaystyle{{}^{E}\mathcal{O}}:={{}^{E}\mathcal{O}}[{\not{U}}]+{{}^{E}\mathcal{O}}[\mu]+{{}^{E}\mathcal{O}}[\tr\chi]+{{}^{E}\mathcal{O}}[\widehat{\chi}]+{{}^{E}\mathcal{O}}[\eta],

where

\displaystyle\big({{}^{E}\mathcal{O}}[{\not{U}}]\big)^{2}:=

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{s}[{\not{U}}]+\sup_{S\subseteq V}|r^{\frac{s+3}{2}}\not{\nabla}{\not{U}}|_{2,S}+\mathcal{E}_{s-{\delta_{B}},{\delta_{B}}}^{1}[{\not{U}}]+\mathcal{F}_{s-{\delta_{B}},{\delta_{B}}}^{1}[{\not{U}}],

\displaystyle\big({{}^{E}\mathcal{O}}[\mu]\big)^{2}:=

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{1,s-1}[\mu]+\mathcal{F}_{s-{\delta_{B}},{\delta_{B}}}^{1}[\mu],

\displaystyle\big({{}^{E}\mathcal{O}}[\tr\chi]\big)^{2}:=

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{s-2}^{1}[\widecheck{\tr\chi}]+\mathcal{F}_{s-2-{\delta_{B}},{\delta_{B}}}^{2}[\widecheck{\tr\chi}]+\mathcal{D}^{(\infty)}_{-1,s-1}\left[\tr\chi-\frac{2}{r}\right],

\displaystyle\big({{}^{E}\mathcal{O}}[\widehat{\chi}]\big)^{2}:=

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{s-2}^{1}[\widehat{\chi}]+\mathcal{F}_{s-2-{\delta_{B}},{\delta_{B}}}^{2}[\widehat{\chi}],

\displaystyle\big({{}^{E}\mathcal{O}}[\eta]\big)^{2}:=

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{-1,s-1}^{1}[\eta]+\mathcal{F}_{s-2-{\delta_{B}},{\delta_{B}}}^{2}[\eta].

Next, we define

\displaystyle{{}^{I}\mathcal{O}}:={{}^{I}\mathcal{O}}[{\not{U}}]+{{}^{I}\mathcal{O}}[\mu]+{{}^{I}\mathcal{O}}[\tr\chi]+{{}^{I}\mathcal{O}}[\widehat{\chi}]+{{}^{I}\mathcal{O}}[\eta],

where

\displaystyle\big({{}^{I}\mathcal{O}}[{\not{U}}]\big)^{2}:=

\displaystyle{{}^{i}\mathcal{D}}_{-2,s+2}[{\not{U}}]+\sup_{S\subseteq{{}^{i}V}}|ru^{\frac{s+1}{2}}\not{\nabla}{\not{U}}|_{2,S}+{{}^{i}\mathcal{E}}_{0,s}^{1}[{\not{U}}]+{{}^{i}\mathcal{F}}_{0,s}^{1}[{\not{U}}],

\displaystyle\big({{}^{I}\mathcal{O}}[\mu]\big)^{2}:=

\displaystyle{{}^{i}\mathcal{D}}_{-2,s+2}[\mu]+{{}^{i}\mathcal{F}}_{0,s}^{1}[\mu],

\displaystyle\big({{}^{I}\mathcal{O}}[\tr\chi]\big)^{2}:=

\displaystyle{{}^{i}\mathcal{D}}_{-3,s+1}^{1}[\widecheck{\tr\chi}]+{{}^{i}\mathcal{F}}_{-2,s}^{2}[\widecheck{\tr\chi}]+{{}^{i}\mathcal{D}^{(\infty)}_{-3,s+1}}\left[\tr\chi-\frac{2}{r}\right],

\displaystyle\big({{}^{I}\mathcal{O}}[\widehat{\chi}]\big)^{2}:=

\displaystyle{{}^{i}\mathcal{D}}_{-3,s+1}^{1}[\widehat{\chi}]+{{}^{i}\mathcal{F}}_{-2,s}^{2}[\widehat{\chi}],

\displaystyle\big({{}^{I}\mathcal{O}}[\eta]\big)^{2}:=

\displaystyle{{}^{i}\mathcal{D}}^{1}_{-3,s+1}[\eta]+{{}^{i}\mathcal{F}}_{-2,s}^{2}[\eta].

We also define

\displaystyle\big({{}^{T}\mathcal{O}}\big)^{2}:=\big({{}^{T}\mathcal{O}}[\eta]\big)^{2}:=\sup_{p\in[-2,-1]}{{}^{i}\mathcal{D}}_{p,s-p}[\not{\nabla}_{T}\eta].

Finally, we denote

\mathcal{O}:={{}^{E}\mathcal{O}}+{{}^{I}\mathcal{O}}+{{}^{T}\mathcal{O}}.

Remark 3.5
.

By definition, the norms
\mathcal{O}
and
\mathcal{K}
control
\delta
,
\epsilon
,
\kappa
,
\nabla\log\phi
,
\eta
and
\chi
. Moreover, we have from (
2.14
)

\displaystyle\underline{\chi}_{AB}

\displaystyle=-\chi_{AB}-2k_{AB},\qquad\qquad\;\underline{\xi}_{A}=\not{\nabla}_{A}\log\phi+\epsilon_{A}-\eta_{A},

\displaystyle 2\omega

\displaystyle=-\nabla_{N}\log\phi+\delta,\qquad\qquad\,2\underline{\omega}=\nabla_{N}\log\phi+\delta,

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{A}\log a

\displaystyle=\eta_{A}-\epsilon_{A},\qquad\qquad\qquad\;\;\;\,\zeta_{A}=\epsilon_{A},

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle=0,\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\;\;\,\underline{\eta}_{A}=\not{\nabla}_{A}\log\phi-\epsilon_{A}.

Thus, the norms
\mathcal{O}
and
\mathcal{K}
control all the connection coefficients.

3.2.4
\mathcal{O}_{(0)}
,
\mathcal{K}_{(0)}
and
\mathfrak{R}_{(0)}
norms (initial data)

We define the curvature flux in the initial layer region
\mathcal{V}_{(0)}
introduced in (
2.9
):

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{R}_{(0)})^{2}:=\sup_{t\in[0,2]}\int_{\Sigma_{t}}r^{s}\left|{\mathfrak{d}}^{\leq 1}\left(\alpha,\beta,\rho,\sigma,\underline{\beta},\underline{\alpha}\right)\right|^{2},\qquad{\mathfrak{d}}:=\{r\not{\nabla}_{3},r\not{\nabla}_{4},r\not{\nabla}\}.

Next, we define the norms of null connection coefficients in the initial layer
\mathcal{V}_{(0)}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{(0)}:=\sum_{\Gamma\in\{\eta,\widecheck{\tr\chi},\widehat{\chi}\}}\mathcal{O}_{(0)}[\Gamma]+\sup_{S\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{(0)}}\left|r^{\frac{s+1}{2}}\left(\tr\chi-\frac{2}{r}\right)\right|_{\infty,S}+\sup_{\Sigma_{0}}|r+u|,

where

\displaystyle(\mathcal{O}_{(0)}[\Gamma])^{2}:=\sup_{t\in[0,2]}\int_{\Sigma_{t}}r^{s-2}|{\mathfrak{d}}^{\leq 2}\Gamma|^{2}+\sup_{S\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{(0)}}|r^{\frac{s+1}{2}}{\mathfrak{d}}^{\leq 2}\Gamma|_{\infty,S}^{2}.

We also define the norms of maximal connection coefficients in
\mathcal{V}_{(0)}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{(0)}:=\sum_{k\in\{\epsilon,\delta,\widehat{\kappa},\nabla\phi\}}\mathcal{K}_{(0)}[k]+\sup_{S\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{(0)}}|r^{\frac{s-1}{2}}(\phi-1)|_{\infty,S},

where

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{K}_{(0)}[k]\big)^{2}:=\sup_{t\in[0,2]}\int_{\Sigma_{t}}r^{s-2}|{\mathfrak{d}}^{\leq 2}k|^{2}.

3.3
Main theorem

We are ready to state a precise version of our main theorem.

Theorem 3.6

(Main Theorem, version 2)
.

Consider an initial data set
(\Sigma_{0},g,k)
,
(s,2)
–asymptotically flat in the sense of Definition
1.2
with
1<s\leq 2
. Assume that we have the following control in the initial layer region
\mathcal{V}_{(0)}
:

\mathcal{O}_{(0)}\leq\varepsilon_{0},\qquad\mathcal{K}_{(0)}\leq\varepsilon_{0},\qquad\mathfrak{R}_{(0)}\leq\varepsilon_{0},

(3.5)

where
\mathcal{O}_{(0)}
,
\mathcal{K}_{(0)}
and
\mathfrak{R}_{(0)}
are defined in Section
3.2.4
and
\varepsilon_{0}>0
is a small enough constant.

Then, the initial layer
\mathcal{V}_{(0)}
has a unique development
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
in its future domain of dependence with the following properties:

1.

(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
can be foliated by a maximal-null foliation
(t,u)
. Moreover, the outgoing cones
C_{u}
are complete for all
u\in\mathbb{R}
.

2.

The norms
\mathcal{O}
,
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{R}
defined in Sections
3.2.1
–
3.2.3
satisfy

\mathcal{O}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0},\qquad\mathcal{K}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0},\qquad\mathcal{R}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}.

(3.6)

Remark 3.7
.

The lower bound
s>1
is almost sharp in the following sense. Denoting
\Gamma
the connection coefficients,
R
the curvature components and
\mu=3,4
, we have from Propositions
2.17
and
2.18
, schematically,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\not{\nabla}_{\mu}\Gamma&=\not{\nabla}\Gamma+R+\Gamma\cdot\Gamma,\\
\not{\nabla}_{\mu}R&=\not{\nabla}R+\Gamma\cdot R.\end{split}

(3.7)

Also, we have from (
1.6
) that the order of decay of
\Gamma
is
-\frac{s+1}{2}
while that of
R
is
-\frac{s+3}{2}
. Thus, since the nonlinear terms should have better order of decay than the linear terms, we should have in view of (
3.7
)

\displaystyle\frac{s+1}{2}+\frac{s+1}{2}>\frac{s+3}{2},\qquad\frac{s+1}{2}+\frac{s+3}{2}>\frac{s+5}{2},

where both restrictions are equivalent to
s>1
.

The proof of Theorem
3.6
is given in Section
3.5
. It hinges on three theorems stated in Section
3.4
, concerning estimates for
\mathcal{O}
,
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{R}
norms.

We choose
\varepsilon_{0}
small enough such that

\varepsilon:=\varepsilon_{0}^{\frac{2}{3}},\qquad\quad 0<\varepsilon\ll 1,

where
\varepsilon
will be the smallness constant involved in bootstrap assumptions. Here,
A\ll B
means that
CA<B
where
C
is the largest universal constant among all the constants involved in the proof via
\lesssim
.

3.4
Main intermediate results

The following three theorems are the main intermediate results in the proof of Theorem
3.6
.

Theorem 3.8
.

Assume that

\mathfrak{R}_{(0)}\leq\varepsilon_{0},\qquad\mathcal{O}\leq\varepsilon,\qquad\mathcal{K}\leq\varepsilon,\qquad\mathcal{R}\leq\varepsilon.

(3.8)

Then, we have

\mathcal{R}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}.

(3.9)

Theorem
3.8
is proved in Section
5
. The proof is based on the
r^{p}
–weighted estimate method introduced by Dafermos and Rodnianski in
[
6
]
and applied to the Bianchi equations.

Theorem 3.9
.

Assume that

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\leq\varepsilon,\qquad\mathcal{K}\leq\varepsilon,\qquad\mathcal{R}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}.

(3.10)

Then, we have

\mathcal{K}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}.

(3.11)

Theorem
3.9
is proved in Section
6
. The proof is done by applying elliptic estimates on maximal hypersurfaces
\Sigma_{t}
.

Theorem 3.10
.

Assume that

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{(0)}\leq\varepsilon_{0},\qquad\mathcal{O}\leq\varepsilon,\qquad\mathcal{K}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0},\qquad\mathcal{R}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}.

(3.12)

Then, we have

\mathcal{O}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}.

(3.13)

Theorem
3.10
is proved in Section
7
. The proof is done by integrating transport equations along the outgoing null cones and applying elliptic estimates on
2
–spheres of the maximal-null foliation of the spacetime
\mathcal{M}
.

3.5
Proof of the main theorem

We now use Theorems
3.8
–
3.10
to prove Theorem
3.6
.

Definition 3.11
.

We denote
\mathcal{U}
the set of values
t_{*}
such that the spacetime

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{t_{*}}:=V_{t_{*}}\cup\mathcal{V}_{(0)}=J^{-}(\Sigma_{t_{*}})\cap J^{+}(\Sigma_{0})

associated with the maxmimal-null foliation
(t,u)
defined in Section
2.1
satisfies the following bounds:

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\leq\varepsilon,\qquad\mathcal{K}\leq\varepsilon,\qquad\mathcal{R}\leq\varepsilon.

(3.14)

The assumption (
3.5
) implies that (
3.14
) holds if
t_{*}\in[0,2]
. So, we have
\mathcal{U}\neq\emptyset
. Next, we define
t_{*}
to be the supremum of the set
\mathcal{U}
. We want to prove
t_{*}=+\infty
. We assume by contradiction that
t_{*}<\infty
. In particular we may assume
t_{*}\in\mathcal{U}
. We have from (
3.5
)

\mathcal{O}_{(0)}\leq\varepsilon_{0},\qquad\mathcal{K}_{(0)}\leq\varepsilon_{0},\qquad\mathfrak{R}_{(0)}\leq\varepsilon_{0}.

(3.15)

Applying Theorems
3.8
,
3.9
and
3.10
one by one in that order, we obtain

\mathcal{R}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0},\qquad\mathcal{K}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0},\qquad\mathcal{O}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}.

(3.16)

By local existence, we can extend
V_{t_{*}}
to
\wideparen{V}:={V}_{t_{*}+\nu}
for a
\nu>0
sufficiently small. We denote
\wideparen{\mathcal{O}}
,
\wideparen{\mathcal{K}}
and
\wideparen{\mathcal{R}}
the norms in the extended region
\wideparen{V}
. We have

\wideparen{\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0},\qquad\wideparen{\mathcal{K}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0},\qquad\wideparen{\mathcal{R}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0},

as a consequence of (
3.16
) and local existence in
t_{*}\leq t\leq t_{*}+\nu
. We deduce that
\wideparen{V}
satisfies (
3.14
), which is a contradiction. Thus, we have
t_{*}=+\infty
, which implies property 1 of Theorem
3.6
. Moreover, we have

\mathcal{O}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0},\qquad\mathcal{K}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0},\qquad\mathcal{R}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0},\quad\mbox{ in }\;\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{V}_{\infty},

(3.17)

which implies property 2 of Theorem
3.6
. This concludes the proof of Theorem
3.6
.

4
Bootstrap assumptions and first consequences

In the rest of the paper, we always assume the following bootstrap assumptions:

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\leq\varepsilon,\qquad\mathcal{K}\leq\varepsilon,\qquad\mathcal{R}\leq\varepsilon.

(4.1)

In this section, we derive first consequences of (
4.1
) in the region
V_{t_{*}}
. In the sequel, the results of this section will be used frequently without explicitly mentioning them.

4.1
Schematic notation
\Gamma_{g}
,
\Gamma_{b}
and
\Gamma_{w}

We introduce the following schematic notations.

Definition 4.1
.

We divide the Ricci coefficients into three parts:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{g}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\widecheck{\tr\chi},\,\widecheck{\tr\underline{\chi}},\,\widehat{\chi},\,\omega,\,\underline{\omega},\,\epsilon,\,\delta,\,\nabla\log\phi,\,\zeta,\,\underline{\eta}\,\right\},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{b}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\eta,\,\not{\nabla}\log a,\,\underline{\xi},\,\tr\chi-\frac{2}{r}\right\},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{w}

\displaystyle:=\{\widehat{\underline{\chi}},\,\widehat{\kappa},\,\widehat{\theta}\}.

We also denote:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{g}^{(1)}

\displaystyle:=(r\not{\nabla})^{\leq 1}\Gamma_{g}\cup\{r\alpha,r\beta,r\rho,r\sigma,r{\not{U}}\},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{b}^{(1)}

\displaystyle:=(r\not{\nabla})^{\leq 1}\Gamma_{b}\cup\{r\mu\},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{w}^{(1)}

\displaystyle:=(r\not{\nabla})^{\leq 1}\Gamma_{w}\cup\{r\underline{\beta}\},

and

\displaystyle\Gamma_{i}^{(2)}:=(r\not{\nabla})^{\leq 1}\Gamma_{i}^{(1)},\quad\mbox{ where }\;i=g,b,w.

Lemma 4.2
.

We have the following estimates:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{s-2}^{1}[\Gamma_{g}]\lesssim\varepsilon^{2},\qquad\mathcal{D}_{-1,s-1}^{1}[\Gamma_{b}]\lesssim\varepsilon^{2},\qquad\mathcal{D}_{-1,s-1}[\Gamma_{w}]\lesssim\varepsilon^{2},\qquad\mathcal{D}_{s-3,1}^{1}[\Gamma_{w}]\lesssim\varepsilon^{2},

and

{}^{i}D^{(\infty)}_{-3,s+1}[\Gamma_{g},\Gamma_{b},\Gamma_{w}]\lesssim\varepsilon^{2},\qquad{{}^{i}\mathcal{D}}^{1}_{-2,s}[\Gamma_{g},\Gamma_{b},\Gamma_{w}]\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}.

Moreover, we have

\mathcal{F}_{s-2-{\delta_{B}},{\delta_{B}}}^{2}[\Gamma_{g},\Gamma_{b},\Gamma_{w}]\lesssim\varepsilon^{2},

(4.2)

where
{\delta_{B}}
is introduced in (
3.4
).

Proof.

It follows directly from (
2.14
), (
4.1
) and Definitions
3.2
and
4.1
.
∎

Lemma 4.3
.

For two quantities
X_{(1)}
and
X_{(2)}
, we denote

\displaystyle X_{(1)}\preceq X_{(2)}

if
X_{(1)}^{(q)}
decays better than
X_{(2)}^{(q)}
for any
q=0,1,2
. Then, throughout this paper, we have

\displaystyle\alpha\preceq\beta\preceq(\rho,\sigma)\preceq\underline{\beta}\preceq\underline{\alpha},\qquad\quad\Gamma_{g}\preceq\Gamma_{b}\preceq\Gamma_{w}.

Proof.

It is a direct consequence of (
4.1
).
∎

Remark 4.4
.

In the sequel, we choose the following conventions:

•

For a quantity
h
satisfying the same or even better decay and regularity as
\Gamma_{i}
, for
i=g,b,w
, we write

h\in\Gamma_{i},\quad i=g,b,w.

•

For a sum of schematic notations, we ignore the terms which have same or even better decay and regularity. For example, we write

\Gamma_{g}+\Gamma_{b}=\Gamma_{b},\qquad\Gamma_{b}+\Gamma_{w}=\Gamma_{w},\qquad\left(\frac{{\langle u\rangle}}{{\langle u\rangle}+r}\right)^{\frac{s-1}{2}}\Gamma_{b}=\Gamma_{g}.

•

Since
\Gamma_{w}
has better decay and regularity than
\Gamma_{b}^{(1)}
, we write

\displaystyle\Gamma_{w}=\Gamma_{b}^{(1)}.

•

For a quantity
h
satisfying the same or even better decay and regularity than the curvature component
R^{(q)}
, we write

h=R^{(q)}.

For example, we have

r^{-1}\Gamma_{w}^{(1)}=\underline{\alpha}^{(0)},\qquad\Gamma_{b}\cdot\rho=\Gamma_{b}\cdot\underline{\beta},\qquad\Gamma_{g}\cdot\beta+\Gamma_{g}\cdot\rho=\Gamma_{g}\cdot\rho.

4.2
Commutation identities in schematic form

Proposition 4.5
.

We have the following simple schematic consequences of the commutator identities

\displaystyle[r\not{\nabla},\not{\nabla}_{4}]

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{g}\cdot r\not{\nabla}+\Gamma_{g}\cdot r\not{\nabla}_{4}+\Gamma_{g}^{(1)},

\displaystyle[r\not{\nabla},\not{\nabla}_{3}]

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{w}\cdot r\not{\nabla}+\Gamma_{b}\cdot r\not{\nabla}_{4}+\Gamma_{b}\cdot r\not{\nabla}_{3}+\Gamma_{w}^{(1)},

\displaystyle[\not{\nabla}_{3},\not{\nabla}_{4}]

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{b}\cdot\not{\nabla}+\Gamma_{g}\cdot\not{\nabla}_{3}+\Gamma_{g}\cdot\not{\nabla}_{4}+r^{-1}\Gamma_{g}^{(1)}.

Proof.

It follows directly from Lemma
2.19
and (
2.14
).
∎

Corollary 4.6
.

We have the following commutator identities:

\displaystyle[\not{\nabla}_{T},\not{\nabla}_{4}]

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{b}\cdot\not{\nabla}+\Gamma_{g}\cdot\not{\nabla}_{T}+\Gamma_{g}\cdot\not{\nabla}_{4}+r^{-1}\Gamma_{g}^{(1)},

\displaystyle[\not{\nabla}_{T},\not{\nabla}_{3}]

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{b}\cdot\not{\nabla}+\Gamma_{g}\cdot\not{\nabla}_{T}+\Gamma_{g}\cdot\not{\nabla}_{4}+r^{-1}\Gamma_{g}^{(1)},

\displaystyle[\not{\nabla}_{T},\not{\nabla}]

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{w}\cdot\not{\nabla}+\Gamma_{b}\cdot\not{\nabla}_{T}+\Gamma_{b}\cdot\not{\nabla}_{4}+r^{-1}\Gamma_{w}^{(1)}.

Proof.

It follows directly from Proposition
4.5
and the fact that
2T=e_{4}+e_{3}
.
∎

4.3
Main equations in schematic form

Proposition 4.7
.

We have the null structure equations:

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\eta

\displaystyle=r^{-1}\Gamma_{b}^{(1)},

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\widehat{\chi}

\displaystyle=r^{-1}\Gamma_{g}^{(1)},\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\;\,\,\not{\nabla}_{3}\widehat{\chi}=r^{-1}\Gamma_{b}^{(1)},

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\tr\chi+\frac{1}{2}(\tr\chi)^{2}

\displaystyle=r^{-1}\Gamma_{g},\qquad\qquad\;\,\,\not{\nabla}_{3}\tr\chi+\frac{1}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}\,\tr\chi=r^{-1}\Gamma_{b}^{(1)},

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\widehat{\underline{\chi}}

\displaystyle=-\underline{\alpha}+r^{-1}\Gamma_{b}^{(1)},\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\not{\nabla}_{4}\widehat{\underline{\chi}}=r^{-1}\Gamma_{w}^{(1)},

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\tr\underline{\chi}+\frac{1}{2}(\tr\underline{\chi})^{2}

\displaystyle=r^{-1}\Gamma_{w}^{(1)},\qquad\qquad\not{\nabla}_{4}\tr\underline{\chi}+\frac{1}{2}\tr\chi\,\tr\underline{\chi}=r^{-1}\Gamma_{g}^{(1)},

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\zeta

\displaystyle=r^{-1}\Gamma_{w}^{(1)},\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\;\;\,\not{\nabla}_{4}\zeta=r^{-1}\Gamma_{g}^{(1)},

\displaystyle{\not{\sdiv}\,}\widehat{\chi}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}{\not{U}}-\beta+\Gamma_{g}\cdot\Gamma_{g},\qquad\qquad\qquad\,{\not{\sdiv}\,}\widehat{\underline{\chi}}=r^{-1}\Gamma_{w}^{(1)}.

Proof.

It follows directly from Proposition
2.17
.
∎

Corollary 4.8
.

We have the following identities:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\not{\nabla}_{T}\widehat{\chi}&=r^{-1}\Gamma_{b}^{(1)},\qquad\;\,\,\not{\nabla}_{T}\zeta=r^{-1}\Gamma_{w}^{(1)},\qquad\;\,\,\not{\nabla}_{T}\widehat{\underline{\chi}}=\underline{\alpha}^{(0)},\\
\not{\nabla}_{T}\tr\chi&=r^{-1}\Gamma_{b}^{(1)},\quad\;\,\not{\nabla}_{T}\tr\underline{\chi}=r^{-1}\Gamma_{w}^{(1)}.\end{split}

(4.3)

Proof.

It follows directly from Proposition
4.7
and the fact that
2T=e_{4}+e_{3}
.
∎

Lemma 4.9
.

We have the following Bianchi equations for
((\wideparen{\rho},\wideparen{\sigma}),\underline{\beta})
:

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\underline{\beta}+\tr\chi\,\underline{\beta}

\displaystyle=\not{d}_{1}^{*}(\wideparen{\rho},\wideparen{\sigma})+\Gamma_{w}\cdot({\not{U}},\beta)+r^{-1}\Gamma_{g}\cdot\Gamma_{w}^{(1)},

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}(\wideparen{\rho},\wideparen{\sigma})+\frac{3}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}(\wideparen{\rho},\wideparen{\sigma})

\displaystyle=-\not{d}_{1}\underline{\beta}+r^{-1}(\Gamma_{b}\cdot\Gamma_{w})^{(1)}.

Proof.

We have from Proposition
4.7

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\widehat{\underline{\chi}}=-\underline{\alpha}+r^{-1}\Gamma_{b}^{(1)},\qquad\not{\nabla}_{3}\widehat{\chi}=r^{-1}\Gamma_{b}^{(1)}.

Hence, we have from Proposition
2.18
and Remark
4.4

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\wideparen{\rho}+\frac{3}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}\,\wideparen{\rho}

\displaystyle=\not{\nabla}_{3}\rho+\frac{3}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}\,\rho-\frac{1}{2}\not{\nabla}_{3}(\widehat{\chi}\cdot\widehat{\underline{\chi}})+r^{-1}\Gamma_{g}\cdot\Gamma_{w}

\displaystyle=-{\not{\sdiv}\,}\underline{\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\chi}\cdot\underline{\alpha}+\Gamma_{b}\cdot(\beta,\underline{\beta})-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\chi}\cdot\not{\nabla}_{3}\widehat{\underline{\chi}}-\frac{1}{2}\not{\nabla}_{3}\widehat{\chi}\cdot\widehat{\underline{\chi}}+r^{-1}\Gamma_{g}\cdot\Gamma_{w}

\displaystyle=-{\not{\sdiv}\,}\underline{\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\chi}\cdot\underline{\alpha}+r^{-1}\Gamma_{b}\cdot\Gamma_{w}^{(1)}-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\chi}\cdot(-\underline{\alpha}+r^{-1}\Gamma_{b}^{(1)})+r^{-1}\Gamma_{b}^{(1)}\cdot\Gamma_{w}

\displaystyle=-{\not{\sdiv}\,}\underline{\beta}+r^{-1}(\Gamma_{b}\cdot\Gamma_{w})^{(1)}

as stated. The derivation of the equation of
\wideparen{\sigma}
is similar and left to the reader.

Next, we have from (
2.18
), (
3.2
), (
3.3
) and Proposition
2.18

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\underline{\beta}+\tr\chi\,\underline{\beta}

\displaystyle=d_{1}^{*}(\rho,\sigma)+\Gamma_{w}\cdot\beta+\Gamma_{g}\cdot(\rho,\sigma,\underline{\beta})

\displaystyle=d_{1}^{*}(\wideparen{\rho},\wideparen{\sigma})+\frac{1}{2}d_{1}^{*}(\widehat{\chi}\cdot\widehat{\underline{\chi}},\widehat{\chi}\wedge\widehat{\underline{\chi}})+\Gamma_{w}\cdot\beta+\Gamma_{g}\cdot(\rho,\sigma,\underline{\beta})

\displaystyle=d_{1}^{*}(\wideparen{\rho},\wideparen{\sigma})+\Gamma_{w}\cdot({\not{U}},\beta)+r^{-1}\Gamma_{g}\cdot\Gamma_{w}^{(1)}

as stated. This concludes the proof of Proposition
4.9
.
∎

Proposition 4.10
.

The Bianchi equations take the following form:

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}\,\alpha

\displaystyle=-2\not{d}_{2}^{*}\beta+\Gamma_{g}\cdot\rho+\Gamma_{b}\cdot\beta,

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\beta+2\tr\chi\,\beta

\displaystyle=\not{d}_{2}\alpha+\Gamma_{g}\cdot\rho,

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\beta+\tr\underline{\chi}\,\beta

\displaystyle=-\not{d}_{1}^{*}(\rho,-\sigma)+\Gamma_{g}\cdot\underline{\beta}+\Gamma_{b}\cdot\rho,

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}(\rho,-\sigma)+\frac{3}{2}\tr\chi(\rho,-\sigma)

\displaystyle=\not{d}_{1}\beta+\Gamma_{w}\cdot\beta,

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}(\wideparen{\rho},\wideparen{\sigma})+\frac{3}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}(\wideparen{\rho},\wideparen{\sigma})

\displaystyle=-\not{d}_{1}\underline{\beta}+r^{-1}(\Gamma_{b}\cdot\Gamma_{w})^{(1)},

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\underline{\beta}+\tr\chi\,\underline{\beta}

\displaystyle=\not{d}_{1}^{*}(\wideparen{\rho},\wideparen{\sigma})+\Gamma_{w}\cdot({\not{U}},\beta)+r^{-1}\Gamma_{g}\cdot\Gamma_{w}^{(1)},

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\underline{\beta}+2\tr\underline{\chi}\,\underline{\beta}

\displaystyle=-\not{d}_{2}\underline{\alpha}+\Gamma_{b}\cdot\underline{\alpha},

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\underline{\alpha}+\frac{1}{2}\tr\chi\,\underline{\alpha}

\displaystyle=2\not{d}_{2}^{*}\underline{\beta}+\Gamma_{g}\cdot\underline{\alpha}+\Gamma_{w}\cdot\rho.

Proof.

It follows directly from Proposition
2.18
and Lemma
4.9
.
∎

Corollary 4.11
.

We have the following identities:

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{T}\alpha

\displaystyle=r^{-1}\beta^{(1)},\qquad\qquad\qquad\;\;\not{\nabla}_{T}\beta=r^{-1}\rho^{(1)}+\Gamma_{g}\cdot\underline{\beta},

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{T}(\rho,\sigma)

\displaystyle=r^{-1}\underline{\beta}^{(1)}+\Gamma_{g}\cdot\underline{\alpha},\qquad\quad\not{\nabla}_{T}\underline{\beta}=r^{-1}\underline{\alpha}^{(1)}.

Proof.

It follows directly from Proposition
4.10
and the fact that
2T=e_{4}+e_{3}
.
∎

4.4
Elliptic estimates in
2D
–geometry

Throughout Section
4.4
, we assume that
t\geq 0
and
u\in\mathbb{R}
are fixed constants and we denote
S:=S(t,u)
the leaf of the maximal-null foliation constructed in Section
2.1.2
.

Proposition 4.12

(
L^{p}
estimates for Hodge systems)
.

The following statements hold for all
p\in(1,+\infty)
:

1.

Let
\phi\in\mathfrak{s}_{0}
be a solution to
\not{\Delta}\phi=f
. Then we have

\displaystyle|\not{\nabla}^{2}\phi|_{p,S}+r^{-1}|\not{\nabla}\phi|_{p,S}+r^{-2}|\widecheck{\phi}|_{p,S}\lesssim|f|_{p,S}.

2.

Let
\xi\in\mathfrak{s}_{1}
be a solution to
\not{d}_{1}\xi=(f,f_{*})
. Then we have

\displaystyle|\not{\nabla}\xi|_{p,S}+r^{-1}|\xi|_{p,S}\lesssim|(f,f_{*})|_{p,S}.

3.

Let
U\in\mathfrak{s}_{2}
be a solution to
\not{d}_{2}U=f
